EMIT: Product(source=None, manufacturer=None, name=u'Impressions: ddHiFi C2023 and C100 travel cases', sku=None, ssid=u'impressions-ddhifi-c2023-and-c100-travel-cases', description=None, category=u'Accessories', url=u'https://theheadphonelist.com/impressions-ddhifi-c2023-and-c100-travel-cases/', properties=[], reviews="[Review(title=u'Impressions: ddHiFi C2023 and C100 travel cases', date=u'2023-09-15', product=None, ssid=u'impressions-ddhifi-c2023-and-c100-travel-cases', url=u'https://theheadphonelist.com/impressions-ddhifi-c2023-and-c100-travel-cases/', click_url=None, super=None, type='pro', authors=[Person(name=u'Guy Lerner', email=None, url=u'https://theheadphonelist.com/author/gler/', ssid=u'gler')], properties=[ReviewProperty(name=None, value=u'Guangzhou DD Electronics Technology Co., better known as ddHiFi , has grown from a small Chinese-based company making connectors and adapters for the likes of FiiO and Oppo in 2017, to a fully-fledged audiophile accessory maker today. I\\u2019ve had the pleasure of using many of the company\\u2019s products over the years, including some of the first travel cases designed specifically for portable audio enthusiasts. Earlier this year, DD released a series of updated travel cases that incorporate many of those early innovations. The 2023 travel case vintage, called C2023 carry case for audiophiles, is like a miniaturised utility bag for those who want to take their valuable audio gear on walks, hikes, city trips, or even cross-country and international travel. The C100 earphone carrying case, made from the same materials as the C2023 case, is a double-sided, oversized pouch for carrying both IEMs and smaller accessories, and can be cleverly attached to the C2023 to create a pseudo-expandable carry system for longer trips. Both cases are made from sturdy, rugged nylon material, and feature cowhide leather for added protection \\u2013 and style . But what makes these cases different, and particularly useful, is the careful, clever design of pockets, pouches and expandable storage that goes beyond accommodating audio gear, making them useful for different activities and even different gear. Let\\u2019s take a closer look. C2023 The C2023 carry case for audiophiles is a shoulder/hip carry bag made of 1680D double-strand nylon and black cowhide leather, lined with 150D polyester, and finished with genuine YKK metal slider zippers. The nylon is soft yet rugged to the touch, with a deep blue hue that gives it a functional and handsome rather than flashy look. Weighing in at 390g with the included shoulder strap, C2023 measures 20cm x 14cm x 18.5cm, and is priced at $59.99 direct from ddHiFi\\u2019s Aliexpress store, Linsoul , or Amazon . With double-strand nylon on the outside, reinforced with cowhide leather on contact surfaces and padding foam in crush-sensitive areas, C2023 feels safe and secure even before you load it up with gear. It has a reassuring heft in hand, but isn\\u2019t heavy or overly bulky. In fact, it feels much more compact than it looks. Where ddHiFi\\u2019s 2022 design was rather stocky and fat, C2023 is taller and slimmer, making it more versatile for carrying today\\u2019s full-size transportable DAPs (like iBasso\\u2019s DX300 MAX) with ease, and can expand further for practical carries, like bottled water for instance. This makes C2023 much more than just a hi-fi gear bag, and depending on what you\\u2019re carrying, I can see it doubling as an everyday carry bag for students, businesspeople, and other busy commuters. C2023 features different options for carrying \\u2013 or even just storing \\u2013 your high-value items. On short trips, I can load it up with a full-size DAP (either the aforementioned DX300 MAX or the equally-bulky HiBy RS8) in the main compartment, with room to spare for an earphone case, additional earphones and cables in the front pouch, a small wallet with cash and cards, a large smartphone, and all the cables, accessories, cards and chargers I might need. Going on a hike? The rear pouch features a u-shaped zipper seam that expands the pouch to hold a water bottle or compact umbrella, leaving the main compartment for a DAP or phone, earphones and cables, wallet, cash, and sundries. Inside the main compartment you\\u2019ll also find smaller zippered pockets and slots for various bits and bobs. If you\\u2019ve ever wondered where to put all those loose cables, dongles, eartips, cards, and other accessories you often have to carry with you on a trip, chances are you\\u2019ll find a place to store them. Even fully-loaded, C2023 feels well-balanced because of its flat, padded, leather-lined base. With three D-rings positioned around the case, you can also choose different ways to attach the shoulder strap, carrying the case either over-shoulder or as an angled sling. Importantly, the adjustable strap is just as useful for shorter adults as it is for beanpoles like me. You can also carry the C2023 case on a carabiner attached to a utility belt or backpack, or even inside a larger backpack if you want to keep your pricey gear hidden from view. Whichever way I\\u2019ve carried it, the C2023 always feels nicely balanced, with every compartment easily accessible at all times. Other than the open rear pouch, it also provides enough protection against light rainfall and accidental spills, something to consider when carrying delicate electronic equipment around with you. Overall, the C2023 carry case is a necessary and impressive evolution of ddHiFi\\u2019s early attempts, like the C2020 case with its earphone cable slot but limited padding, and even the larger, stronger but rather awkward C2021 utility case. Of all the cases I\\u2019ve seen on the market for an out-and-about walk with a DAP, IEMs, or even non-audio daily caries, this is the one I\\u2019d get for maximum protection, versatility and quality. C100 Made with the same high-quality double-strand blue nylon, 150D polyester, black leather and metal YKK zippers as its C2023 big brother, the C100 earphone case is much more than just that. Featuring two compartments in a round, clamshell design, you can fit at least two sets of earphones, high-end cables, cards, dongles and other small accessories across its partitioned sections. The smaller top compartment features mesh pockets on the top and bottom for smaller, flatter accessories, with the middle section open for smaller earphones or cables. The bottom compartment is much larger, padded, with enough room for full-size IEMs, cables, dongles, or, if you prefer, multiple smaller accessories. ddHiFi includes a 4-way padded divider inside the main compartment that you can use to keep different accessories separated from each other, or keep your IEM earpieces protected. The divider is removable, so you can use the empty compartment for larger equipment too. With a sturdy carabiner built in to one side of the case, the C100 can be thrown in a bag as easily as it can be attached to a belt, or hooked on to one of the D-rings on the C2023. In fact, you if you need to carry more gear than usual, you can hook two C100s to the C2023 for a large but very roomy, modular travel system. C100 is also available to buy for $26 direct from ddHiFi\\u2019s Aliexpress store, Linsoul , or Amazon . Closing thoughts The audiophile accessory market is filling up with many different options for storing and presenting your gear at almost every price point, but still very few options are readily available for carrying your valuable gear safely on the go. In C2023 and C100, ddHiFi has created two such solutions which, depending on your needs, can even serve as all the storage you\\u2019ll need for your gear, whether you work from home or like to take your music with you on walks, hikes and trips. Both are sturdy, fashionable, and expertly made, with obvious attention to detail in both the finishing and design choices. Even if you\\u2019re not in the market for carrying your portable audio system around town, I\\u2019d still rec C2023, if not its smaller sibling, for carrying functional gear on walks and hikes, or even as a daily carry for wallets, phones and other everyday stuff. It\\u2019s quickly become one of my favourite outdoor carry bags \\u2013 and coming from someone like me with a serious (and somewhat embarrassing) bag fetish \\u2013 that\\u2019s high paise indeed. Pros : Cons: Cables. A subject that listeners love to hate, and hate to love. The mere mention of the word \\u201cCables\\u201d in any specialty audiophile forum is bound to attract a chorus of detestations and attestations. In layman\\u2019s terms, an audio signal has to traverse from source to transducer. As it makes the journey from one end to the next, there are a myriad of variables enhancing or detracting from the output signal (conductivity, capacitance, metallurgical purity etc.). Given that the cable is the medium in which a signal travels, I am a cable believer, albeit not a puritanical one. That is the primary reason I chose to return to Astral Acoustics, a cable specialist no stranger to readers on the Headphone List. To save me the effort of rewriting a TLDR summary of the Astral Acoustics brand, here\\u2019s a snapshot of what I previously wrote in my review of their latest \\u2018Mars\\u2019 cable offering: \\u201c Astral Acoustics, a Hong Kong brand founded by the personable and convivial Henry Tik in 2016, is one such brand specialising in the cable space. Astral Acoustics pay close attention to researching cable physics, insulation science and complex metallurgy to explore novel cable formulations. Every critical component has been deliberately curated to squeeze every bit of performance possible out of all imaginable pairings. From the traditional (silver litz etc.) to the experimental (palladium Ag eg.), the titular brand has an adventurous disposition to trying new things. \\u201c As per Henry\\u2019s unrelenting quality control process, every cable that exits his workshop is impartially assessed to meet his exacting specifications before release, which oscillates between the qualitative and the subjective. Rigorous stress testing and subjective pairings with his personal listening equipment help to determine if each cable iteration is ready to go to market. Today, we are paying close attention to one of their legacy offerings in their diverse catalogue of upgrade cables, the Eclipse. Fabricated from 4 strands of 24 AWG Type 4 Litz 5N Silver, the Eclipse is a flagship-level upgrade cable for the luxurious audiophile where nothing is unattainable (for money is no object). Priced at 950 USD (50 USD extra for interchangeable plugs), the Eclipse\\u2019s extravagant price tag is bound to turn some heads. Accordingly, the Eclipse has some heavy expectations to satisfy, and we\\u2019re here to qualitatively determine if the Eclipse. The Eclipse can be purchased directly from Astral Acoustics official web-store . Metallurgy blends the realm of art and hard science, which forms the basis of sound cable engineering. Signal quality is contingent on the crystalline purity of the conductor, in this case, the 24 AWG type 4 Litz 5N silver bundles comprising the core of each braid. Silver has the highest conductivity of all metals, which lends itself well to be used as a hyper-efficient conduit for unimpeded signal travel. On the \\u201cartsy\\u201d end, each cable is furnished with high-quality visual flourishes. In the Eclipse, the deliberate integration of vertically brushed aluminium elements with powder-coated piano-black barrels in the Y-split and termination enhances the overall aesthetics of the entire package, akin to the habillage or intricate details on a hand-finished timepiece. If you\\u2019ve read my previous review on Astral Acoustics\\u2019 latest cable, the Mars, all Astral Acoustics products come packaged in their default cardboard boxes in black, with their official logo and namesake embossed into the top lids in silver. Under the hood, the Eclipse will be neatly wrapped up in a felt protective pouch with an official warranty card. At Eclipse\\u2019s asking price, I would\\u2019ve preferred a more premium packaging experience that differentiated itself from the rest of Astral Acoustics\\u2019 current line-up: a physical means to symbolise the associated prestige and performance that comes with it. Thankfully, the Eclipse dispels that myth, for it magically manages to mitigate the downsides of cable rigidity. Firstly, the heft and girth of each intertwining braid make for a satisfying hand feel, its doubly thick sheathing instilling confidence in its ability to take a beating. It isn\\u2019t a hulking behemoth, but it certainly commands more attention than most IEM cables. What\\u2019s wholly impressive about the Eclipse is how it manages to retain extreme suppleness and malleability in any situation it finds itself in, conforming to rather than limiting your listening habits. Thankfully, it doesn\\u2019t uncontrollably bend or kink in all directions, maintaining its structural integrity in outdoor usage whilst being supple enough to be stowed away without much resistance. As alluded to in the previous section, the Eclipse is beautifully adorned in aluminium hardware, each component machine-finished to a high standard, from the Y-split to the termination. Premium parts like this are a welcomed gesture in a statement product like the Eclipse. As a whole, the Eclipse is a halo product that excels in both build and flexibility without any perceivable pitfalls. A home run . Cables are like fine wines. They can complement or enhance the flavours of gastronomical creations in subtle or assertive ways. If a wine sommelier does a terrible job, counterintuitive pairings can contradict, mask, or harm the delicate taste of intricate dishes. The same rules are extrapolated to audio. Keep in mind that cables are complementary , not revolutionary . No matter how hard you try, you cannot simply rewrite the fundamental base signature of any IEM or headphones. We discuss how the sonic qualities of my reference IEMs are altered through these selective pairings. Before Pairing: The Soranik ION-4X\\u2019s fundamental signature oscillates between neutral-light and sub-bass bombast. Micro-detail precision and dexterous PRAT bolster the ION-4X\\u2019s treble performance, with enough shimmer and edge for porous highs and assertive presence. There is a slight colouration in the overall midrange, with a marginal emphasis on the upper midrange. The lateral width between the L-R channels is expansive and wide, trailing behind Kilobuck\\u2019s flagship performance. Because of the emphatic treble performance and rotund sub-bass flourish, perceived separation and technical distinguishability between instruments and vocal cues are excellent. After Pairing: Right off the bat, the Eclipse manages to inject liveliness and clarity across the full frequency band. The sub-bass shelf on the ION-4X is presented in a less boxy manner, where note-strikes exhibit cleaner definition and timbrally-accurate tones. The confluence between the upper-mids and presence regions appears brighter whilst mitigating some of the \\u201chissiness\\u201d that can emerge on dynamically compressed tracks. The only aberration is the Eclipse\\u2019s ability to subvert our classical expectations of what a \\u201cpure-silver\\u201d cable should sound like. The Eclipse excels in presenting music in a saccharinely sweet manner by softening the edgier notes in the presence region, focusing on softened macro-dynamics and a richer lower-midrange. Even-harmonic tones take precedence. This isn\\u2019t a ruler-flat signature, but it certainly strikes a win-win compromise between toe-tapping musicality and technical virtuosity. This could be seen as a downside. Apples and oranges. Lateral-width staging on the ION-4X is heightened immensely, with improved clarity and note-legibility. Vocal performance is especially strong, with female vocals sounding extra sweet with an analogue tilt. This is what we should expect from cables bearing the flagship moniker. Before Pairing: The IMR Acoustics Dark Matters are an enigmatic pair of earphones. Once touted as the flagship black sheep of their previous line-up (which is constantly evolving based on the brand\\u2019s amorphous ambitions), the IMR Dark Matters has a meaty-and-thick sound that enunciates bass aplomb and bombast, with gobs of sub-bass and an alluring mid-range: courtesy of the Sounion bone-conduction motors in its novel driver architecture. Highs, while retaining a noteworthy amount of micro-detailing, presence is naturally sidelined to focus on hyper-timbral richness and toe-tapping melodies. The Dark Matter\\u2019s default sound signature can be deliberately attenuated based on the brand\\u2019s recommended filter pairings (lower and upper filters) to satisfy the listener\\u2019s preferences. Today, I\\u2019ve opted to use the black-lower and blue-upper for an aggressively V-shaped sound signature. After Pairing: The Eclipse is unironically eclipsed by the IMR Acoustic\\u2019s pulsating bass performance and its accompanied warmth. In these unique circumstances, the Eclipse actually tightens the lower end and downplays the artificially albeit addictive timbral quirks. Sub-bass rumble adopts a more modest approach, with less floor-rattling bass bloom. A cleaner sound signature results in a commendable leap in overall quality and clarity. Make no mistake, this is still a thunderous IEM. Now it\\u2019s hard to subjectively assess the Eclipse\\u2019s qualitative impact on how the Dark Matters\\u2019 bone-conduction drivers perform. To my ears, there is a perceived improvement in speed and tactility in the upper-mid to presence region, where PRAT is accelerated in terms of transients. Thus, there is a fundamental improvement in how vocal melodies or instruments reverberate or resonate in the perceived stage. An incisive and assertive upper-mid band are qualities audiophiles would associate with technically proficient flagship IEMs. As per usual, lateral width and Z-axis height experience a leap in performance, with panning between channels displaying strong imaging and distinguishability between sonic cues. While the Eclipse does enliven the Dark Matter\\u2019s \\u201cdark\\u201d signature by a healthy margin, audio enthusiasts who are hoping to finesse it into a closer-to-reference sound signature would be disappointed. The Eclipse is a halo product symbolising the culmination of Astral Acoustics\\u2019 technical know-how and space-age design language. More importantly, the Eclipse is a daring re-interpretation of how \\u201csilver\\u201d should sound, challenging conventional stereotypes the element attracts. Excelling at the romantic and the technical, the Eclipse is a potent IEM cable spotlighting the best qualities most IEMs bring to the foray. It\\u2019s now clear where their latest Mars cable draws their inspiration. The Eclipse is their crowning jewel. And it\\u2019s unlikely its little siblings will eclipse it for the long foreseeable future. EMIT: Product(source=None, manufacturer=None, name=u'Impressions: ddHiFi DJ44S for Sony Walkman WM1Z', sku=None, ssid=u'impressions-ddhifi-dj44s-for-sony-walkman-wm1z', description=None, category=u'Accessories', url=u'https://theheadphonelist.com/impressions-ddhifi-dj44s-for-sony-walkman-wm1z/', properties=[], reviews="[Review(title=u'Impressions: ddHiFi DJ44S for Sony Walkman WM1Z', date=u'2023-12-25', product=None, ssid=u'impressions-ddhifi-dj44s-for-sony-walkman-wm1z', url=u'https://theheadphonelist.com/impressions-ddhifi-dj44s-for-sony-walkman-wm1z/', click_url=None, super=None, type='pro', authors=[Person(name=u'Guy Lerner', email=None, url=u'https://theheadphonelist.com/author/gler/', ssid=u'gler')], properties=[ReviewProperty(name=None, value=u'I\\u2019d like to thank ddHiFi for supplying us with a sample DJ44S adapter for review; we appreciate your ongoing support.', type='summary'), ReviewProperty(name=None, value=u'Introduction To ground or not to ground \\u2013 that is the question. Unless you\\u2019re an audio engineer or serious enthusiast, you may not understand the ins-and-outs of electrical signal grounding, especially when it comes to digital audio players and balanced cables. Count me among the group of those for whom grounding meant very little, until recently\\u2026 As a long-time user of Sony\\u2019s venerable Walkman WM1Z, I\\u2019ve often wondered about the occasional debate that flares up in audio communities about the grounding (or lack thereof) of the 1Z\\u2019s 4.4mm balanced headphone output. It turns out that Sony engineers decided to use a non-conventional method of signal grounding with both the Walkman 1Z and 1A: via a ground pin in the 3.5mm output port to the chassis . That means that a 4.4mm balanced cable that lacks a dedicated fifth or grounding pin won\\u2019t get the inherent sonic advantages of the 1Z\\u2019s chassis grounding. It actually gets more complicated than that, as I\\u2019ll explain shortly, but for all intents and purposes, let\\u2019s assume the 1Z is generally not grounded with a large number of standard 4.4mm cables. That\\u2019s where the subject of this review, ddHiFi\\u2019s DJ44S 4.4mm ground pin adapter, comes in. It essentially couples the 4.4mm output with a ground pin that\\u2019s inserted into the grounded 3.5mm output port, and therefore passes on the grounding advantage to any 4.4mm cable that\\u2019s used with the adapter in place. Why is this important? Let\\u2019s find out. Keeping grounded Grounding is far more common in the desktop world, where devices connected to AC mains power need a dedicated electrical pathway to reject noise and interference from the grid and other electrical equipment. In a nutshell, grounding creates a reference point (zero volts) for the voltage of the audio signal inside a digital audio player, diverting noise and other electrical interference away from the audio circuit. It\\u2019s called grounding because in the case of mains-connected electrical equipment, the grounding channel usually goes to ground \\u2013 literally. By reducing noise and interference, grounding has several sonic advantages, not least a darker background that creates a perceivably larger soundstage . A cleaner signal also helps improve resolution , and increases the impact of certain frequencies, typically bass . Some people argue that because digital audio players use batteries, the audio signal can\\u2019t actually be \\u2018grounded\\u2019 in the same way as mains-powered equipment. But they can, in several ways, actually. The first (and least practical) is\\xa0connecting the DAP\\u2019s metal chassis to an external ground reference point,\\xa0like a dedicated grounding box or a grounded piece of equipment,\\xa0using a shielded cable.\\xa0This can help divert noise away from the internal circuitry, but doesn\\u2019t really make sense for a device you\\u2019re carrying around with you. Another is by using shielded cables. Cables like PW Audio\\u2019s Orpheus (reviewed here ) and First Times Shielding (reviewed here )\\xa0feature dedicated shielding material to minimise noise pickup from external sources. This is similar to, but not always the same as using balanced connections; simply\\xa0using 4.4mm balanced connections inherently rejects common-mode noise,\\xa0even without perfect grounding. Lastly, and as introduced above, is Sony\\u2019s method: use the chassis itself as a grounding device, but this only works if the 4.4mm balanced cable you\\u2019re using has a grounding pin. Since many don\\u2019t, you\\u2019ll need to find another way to connect your cables to the Walkman\\u2019s chassis. The DJ44S solution ddHiFi\\u2019s DJ44S M1 is\\xa0designed specifically for Sony\\u2019s original WM1Z DAP, with its integrated (but only selectively connected) chassis grounding pins.\\xa0The ddHiFi adapter bridges the gap between standard balanced cables and the DAP\\u2019s grounding mechanism by inserting a grounding pin into the 3.5mm headphone output port, which is then physically connected to a 4.4mm plug inserted into the DAP\\u2019s balanced output. The result: a fully-grounded all-new 4.4mm output port, ready and waiting for your cable of choice. If you\\u2019ve ever seen or used a WM1Z Walkman before, you\\u2019ll know how meticulously it\\u2019s been designed and built, the sheer quality of its gold-plated solid copper chassis spawning the DAP\\u2019s \\u2018Gold Brick\\u2019 nickname. It stands to reason that any adapter you\\u2019d likely want to use with this marvel of industrial engineering should likewise follow the same design language and build quality. DJ44S is not the only WM1Z ground pin adapter on the market, but as far as I know it\\u2019s the only one that preserves the Walkman\\u2019s gold aesthetic as closely as possible. Unlike the WM1Z, the moulded outer shell is made of a lightweight aluminium alloy, anodized in gold to match the colour of the Walkman\\u2019s 24-carat gold plating. Internally, the adapter uses a 25.5awg 6N OCC copper cable and lead-free gold solder to connect an original Pentaconn 4.4mm balanced plug to the DAP\\u2019s 4.4mm output, along with a 24-carat gold-plated ground pin that, as described above, fits into the grounded 3.5mm output port. The ground pin is much shorter in length than the 4.4mm plug, and for good reason: to prevent damaging the plastic 3.5mm port when inserting and removing the adapter. If the pins were the same length, the force placed on the 3.5mm port could potentially cause it to fray over time. Other than this rather clever design detail, everything else is as simple as it seems: plug it in, plug in a 4.4mm cable, enjoy grounded (and balanced) sound output. It even fits onto the WM1Z without having to remove the TPU case I use to protect my Walkman. Sound impressions Playing just a few test tracks back-to-back with and without the adapter in place makes it quite obvious that grounding does, in fact, have an audible effect on sound quality. I tested the DJ44S with a new IEM I happened to have on hand for review, FIR Audio\\u2019s e12 (review coming soon). Out the box and using its stock cable, this IEM is quite bombastic, revealing, and \\u2013 for want of a better word \\u2013 edgy. Some of its tuning quirks push the sound to extremes, which makes it quite easy to hear where the grounding changes occur. With the DJ44S in play, I hear a cleaner sound. The slight grain I picked up in the midrange and treble frequencies using the WM1Z in its ungrounded state is significantly muted with the adapter in place. This results in a less fatiguing sound, and an easier appreciation of the e12\\u2019s inherent resolution. The darker background also makes some details easier to pick out, and stage feels slightly expanded in all dimensions. It\\u2019s important to stress that the changes I describe above are subtle , but because I\\u2019m listening with a high-quality transducer and one of the world\\u2019s best sources, they\\u2019re also easy to pick out and describe. In other words, I don\\u2019t have to strain to hear the differences. If there\\u2019s any downside at all to this adapter, it\\u2019s that you\\u2019ll want to avoid plugging our unplugging your cable while the player is still switched on and with IEMs in your ears. Doing so sent a loud feedback loop through the cable and earphones, making me jump out my skin more than once. I guess all that residual noise has to go somewhere\\u2026 Closing thoughts There are many different layers to achieving great portable sound. Using the best gear, like Sony\\u2019s Walkman WM1Z, is always a good start. But even then there are ways to keep pushing the envelope, and making sure your balanced cables are properly grounded is one of them. Since you can\\u2019t always be sure your cables are grounded \\u2013 or shielded, for that matter \\u2013 ddHiFi\\u2019s $100 DJ44S M1 adapter is basically a shoe-in for WM1Z users. The only reason I\\u2019d consider not buying one is if you feel it adds too much bulk to the player (preventing it from fitting inside a compact pouch, for example), or if you know for sure your cables are already shielded, in which case adding a grounding pin adapter won\\u2019t make a significant difference. Practically-speaking, the adapter also changes the angle of entry for the 4.4mm port, which could affect how flush your IEM cables sit with the player. But all these nitpicks are just that. The adapter is small, light and robust enough to carry with you and use only when required, and looks like a matched custom extension to Sony\\u2019s Gold Brick if you just want to leave it permanently attached. Either way, I recommend the ddHiFi DJ44S without reservation. You can purchase the ddHiFi DJ44S M1 from Linsoul , HiFiGo and Amazon , among other reputable online resellers. Your continued support is much appreciated.', type='summary'), ReviewProperty(name=None, value=u'ddHiFi is no stranger to the portable audio accessories market, and fresh for 2024, the company has released a set of two new carry cases to complement its already-extensive product portfolio. The two cases \\u2013 CZ300 and CZ180 \\u2013 couldn\\u2019t be more different. In fact, the only similarities they share are the dual custom YKK zippers, and the ddHiFi branding. I\\u2019ll be spending significantly more time telling you about the CZ300 because as of today, it\\u2019s displaced all my other storage cases as the most robust and compact carry case for all my gear. CZ300: the complete portable storage solution Based on previous dd case designs, my expectations weren\\u2019t especially high when I first read about the new CZ series. That\\u2019s not to say I don\\u2019t like dd cases \\u2013 quite the opposite. But for someone like me who, as a reviewer, needs to store and move quite a large selection of gear, I always found dd\\u2019s cases slightly too compact for my needs. Not so the CZ300. The minute I opened the box and unwrapped the new canvas-covered case, I knew this was going to be very different to what dd\\u2019s made before. For starters, this is a large case, and specifically large where it counts: height. Too many cases, both hard and soft-covered, are made too short to fit thicker gear (or gear stacks). This is a shortcoming (pun intended) the CZ300, measuring in at 320 \\xd7 220 \\xd7 100mm, easily overcomes. But despite its relative size, the CZ300 still feels very compact, and looks it too with its understated earth tone colourway. The outer case is made of a waterproof canvas material that feels very resilient but also quite smooth to the touch. Unlike previous dd cases that featured large strips of leather for d\\xe9cor, the CZ300 uses minimal leather, mostly for the seams surrounding the dual YKK zipper glides around the top of the case. Overall construction is very sturdy, and even though it\\u2019s not a hardshell case, it still feels like it\\u2019s going to be very protective of the innards. It also weights a solid 800g, and significantly more when fully loaded (as you\\u2019ll soon see), but despite the heft, it maintains its shape very well, and can be set to stand on its wider or narrower sides without issue. A convenient carry handle makes it simple to move around when you\\u2019re ready to go. Opening the case reveals a very clever three-part design comprising a mesh pocket section in the lid, a fabric zippered divider that doubles as a soft but solid surface for resting gear, and a large, spacious inner section with a unique divider system. Starting with the lid section, there are four elasticated mesh pockets that easily store small accessories like audio dongles, tip cases, IEM pouches, cables, and other miscellaneous bits and pieces. Just be mindful not to overload the mesh pockets as thicker items will potentially take away from the storage volume available in the main section. In-between the lid storage and main sections, the woven fabric divider is a clever touch. Not only does it protect both storage sections, it also keeps dirt and dust out of the main storage area when the case is open, and as mentioned earlier, can be used a soft surface on which to rest your gear while working. Because it\\u2019s soft, it also allows for slightly thicker items in both storage areas to press against it \\u2013 rather than against other gear \\u2013 with no risk of damage. Lastly, the main section is where the wow factor comes in. The first thing I noticed when opening up the case was the foam-filled grooves sewn securely to the internal sides of the case. These, it turns out, are used to support a set of flat, covered dividers that slot in to the grooves, allowing you to partition the main section into smaller areas. You get a long divider (with its own foam-filled grooves) that slots in and partitions the main area lengthways, and a set of eight smaller dividers that slot in-between the long divider and the peripheral grooves to create smaller partitions. Using the provided dividers, I was able to create a generous space with two main partitions, and a further ten smaller partitions: eight for IEMs with third-party cables attached, one for my collection of three full-size DAPs, and a small section for filling up with silica gel pouches to keep everything dry. I have to say, this is the most impressive partition system I\\u2019ve seen on any case to date. In my experience, most storage cases use Velcro to affix dividers, and even when the dividers themselves fit neatly into the case, the Velcro feels flimsy and wears down over time. Worse still, some cases use soft dividers, which might be more flexible but end up looking awful, especially when they\\u2019re buttressed with two-way Velcro strips. Still, the CZ300 divider system isn\\u2019t perfect. Because the short dividers are only supplied in one length, they can only be used when the long divider splits the internal cavity into two equal parts (otherwise they\\u2019re too short or too long). Moreover, since you only get eight short dividers, there\\u2019s a limited number of storage spaces you can make before running out of dividers. Also, the tolerance of some of the dividers is a bit off, so they don\\u2019t slot in particularly tightly and can come loose too easily as a result. All that said, the configuration I came up with using the supplied dividers is basically ideal for my personal testbench system. As you can see below, every item is stored neatly, with plenty of room (and importantly, plenty of height), and that goes even for the large flagship DAPs, which I stacked sideways for easy access. Overall, this is the best case I\\u2019ve personally found for storing and easily transporting a large selection of gear. You can even squeeze an iPad Pro in between the lid and the fabric divider, which makes for a complete portable audio and source carry solution. Considering the asking price for the CZ300 is south of $60, that\\u2019s insane value for one of the best all-round, high-capacity, high-quality storage cases on the market today. CZ180: an ultra-compact retro carry If you\\u2019re only looking to carry a small amount of gear with you, or want to protect larger gear (like TOTL DAPs) in individual cases, that\\u2019s where the new CZ180 comes in. There\\u2019s some familial resemblance to the CZ300, especially the dual YKK zippers with their burnt-sienna pull tags on the outside, but other than that, they\\u2019re made for very different purposes and buyers. The exterior of the 200 x 120 x 60mm case is made of a smooth composite two-tone leather, olive green on the lid and light tan on the base. Opening the case reveals a main interior covered by a non-zippered hinged fabric partition, used more for protection than actual function. There\\u2019s no storage space in the lid either. The main compartment is lined in a soft, fuzzy fabric, receptive to the various lengths of soft Velcro dividers provided inside. You can use these dividers to partition the compartment in several ways, so if you get creative it\\u2019s perfectly suitable for carrying three sets of IEMs, a couple of cables, and a dongle or two. You can also use it to carry a mid-sized DAP and a larger IEM/cable combo, or, without the partitions, to carry one full-size flagship DAP (or portable DAC or amp). Aside from my general dislike for soft Velcro dividers, there\\u2019s nothing functionally wrong with the CZ180, and considering its relatively affordable sub-$30 price point, it\\u2019s perfectly conceivable that many buyers will opt for buying more than one to store different items. Like the CZ300, the CZ180 is very well made, with high-quality zippers that won\\u2019t get jammed or scratch your gear, but given the size and space available, its utility is fairly limited. Closing thoughts If you\\u2019re in the market for an all-in-one carry solution that doesn\\u2019t force you to use fixed partitions, and that doesn\\u2019t resort to flimsy Velcro dividers like too many cases do nowadays, pay close attention to the new ddHiFi CZ300. It\\u2019s a really, really good \\u2013 scratch that, really excellent \\u2013 case, and I don\\u2019t hesitate for a second recommending it to all but the most precious of portable audio users, for whom nothing but pure leather and unicorn hair stitching will do. If you just want a simple, partition-able carry case for a large DAP or a few loose items, the CZ180 is an easy choice, but there are many other equally-compelling options, not least from dd, for that sort of carry. There\\u2019s nothing I\\u2019ve personally seen that comes close to the compact cleverness of the CZ300, certainly nothing that doesn\\u2019t cost several multiples of $60 with very little if any additional benefit. EMIT: Product(source=None, manufacturer=None, name=u'ALT-R Juno: Inception \u2013 A Carrying Case', sku=None, ssid=u'alt-r-juno-inception-a-carrying-case', description=None, category=u'Accessories', url=u'https://theheadphonelist.com/alt-r-juno-inception-a-carrying-case-review/', properties=[], reviews="[Review(title=u'ALT-R Juno: Inception \\u2013 A Carrying Case Review', date=u'2024-06-23', product=None, ssid=u'alt-r-juno-inception-a-carrying-case', url=u'https://theheadphonelist.com/alt-r-juno-inception-a-carrying-case-review/', click_url=None, super=None, type='pro', authors=[Person(name=u'Deezel', email=None, url=u'https://theheadphonelist.com/author/deezel/', ssid=u'deezel')], properties=[ReviewProperty(name=None, value=u'Select Comparisons Eletech Luxe Eletech\\u2019s Luxe and Astute are what I usually use to carry my gear. But, with both my Pluto\\u2019s here, ALT-R have thankfully executed them perfectly. In terms of capacity, Eletech have included enough dividers for a maximum of 6 spaces, each large enough to contain 1-2 sets of IEMs and cables (of average size, of course). Or, the more common config would be to omit 1 divider for 4 of those spaces, plus 1 DAP. Conversely, each Pluto has 4 dividers, separating its internal space into 5 spaces. Again, though, with all 4 up, the spaces you\\u2019ll get are very narrow. I typically only use 3 dividers for 4 spaces. And, if you wanted to store a DAP, you\\u2019d have 1 space left for your IEMs and/or cables. So, with both Pluto\\u2019s full, you\\u2019d have a total capacity of 10 IEMs, or 6 IEMs and 1 DAP, or 2 IEMs and 2 DAPs. Taking all that into account, I\\u2019d say the Juno wins out in overall capacity. But, again, the Luxe is far more space-efficient given its size and weight. Eletech Astute The Astute is a smaller version of the Luxe with an included strap, so most of the comparisons I\\u2019ve made above apply here too (with the added note that the Astute fits about 1-2 less sets of IEMs. What I\\u2019d like to talk about here is the shoulder strap. Despite the huge difference in size and weight, I actually find the Juno more comfortable to carry, and that\\u2019s down to the shape and padding of the strap. ALT-R\\u2019s is rounded and well-cushioned, while the Astute has squared edges with almost no padding whatsoever. Despite the case\\u2019s light weight on its own, that strap does dig into the shoulder when the Astute is fully filled. So, I\\u2019d give ALT-R the clear edge there, and I\\u2019d implore Eletech to look into padding for their next strapped case. DD-HiFi C-2019 While Eletech\\u2019s cases are closer peers to ALT-R\\u2019s given their similar-ish stylings, materials and prices, DD-HiFi\\u2019s C-2019 is almost the opposite. It\\u2019s a much more affordable case with cheaper materials, blander aesthetics and a strong, mass-produced feel. It\\u2019d be wildly unfair to compare them apples-to-apples. Besides, DD-HiFi have gone on to produce better, higher-quality cases than this too. This is just the one I happened to have on hand. So, what I\\u2019d like to do instead is highlight some of the clever features DD-HiFi have given the C-2019, and see where ALT-R can potentially improve. First, mesh pockets for tiny accessories and SD cards. Whether it\\u2019s adapters, ICs, tips, or even alcohol wipes, there\\u2019s always merit to having pockets for these oft-neglected items. ALT-R are halfway there with their soft pouches, but including a better way to integrate them into the case would be ideal, which they\\u2019ve now thankfully included. DD-HiFi have also stitched elastic straps to the flooring of their case, so DAPs stay in place inside them. While I think it\\u2019s of less importance than the mesh pockets, these sorts of nifty features are things ALT-R can consider. So, rather than simply compete on a materials and lavishness front, they can become stronger contenders against the ultra-efficient, ultra-pragmatic Van Nuys and DD-HiFi\\u2019s of the world as well. Verdict The Juno (and its two accompanying Pluto\\u2019s) are a cracking first effort from ALT-R. The brand wears its love for lavish materials on its sleeve, fully palpable throughout all three cases inside and out. And, features like the dual-sided opening mechanism, adjustable padding on the strap and the neoprene-infused pouch show a great eye for detail too. While there are definitely ideas here that need some work, such as the amount of Velcro and, most of all, space and accessory management, what Vishy and his team have already done within the first few months of starting shop is nothing short of impressive. With these kinks ironed out and with ALT-R\\u2019s constant endorsement for client customizability, I have no doubt they have it in them to stay and compete for a very, very long time. EMIT: Product(source=None, manufacturer=None, name=u'Eletech Baroque Eartips', sku=None, ssid=u'review-eletech-baroque-eartips', description=None, category=u'Accessories', url=u'https://theheadphonelist.com/review-eletech-baroque-eartips/', properties=[], reviews="[Review(title=u'Review: Eletech Baroque Eartips', date=u'2024-06-24', product=None, ssid=u'review-eletech-baroque-eartips', url=u'https://theheadphonelist.com/review-eletech-baroque-eartips/', click_url=None, super=None, type='pro', authors=[Person(name=u'Guy Lerner', email=None, url=u'https://theheadphonelist.com/author/gler/', ssid=u'gler')], properties=[ReviewProperty(name=None, value=u'It\\u2019s not often that something this small makes such a big difference, but Eletech\\u2019s new Baroque eartips have raised the bar for those small silicone bits we often take for granted in our audio chains. Over the past few months, I\\u2019ve been fortunate to experience and thoroughly enjoy some outstanding IEM cables from Singapore-based Element Technology, better known to you and me as Eletech . But contrary to popular wisdom, it turns out Eric Chong has more than just cables in his repertoire. \\u201c We see ourselves as a boutique audio accessories maker with cables as our core ,\\u201d he told me in a recent conversation. \\u201c We\\u2019ve always been very serious about crafting good products, not just cables, but also other audio accessories like cases and now eartips .\\u201d Baroque is the culmination of more than three years\\u2019 R&D, which started about a year after Eric founded Eletech in 2019, and it\\u2019s taken him this long to narrow down the supply chain and find just the right combination of materials and acoustic design know-how to get it right. Designing Baroque As with everything else Eletech, the entire design process was kept in-house, from material sourcing to testing.\\xa0According to Eric, they\\xa0wanted the creative space to implement their own ideas about ergonomics and acoustics, no matter how tough or bothersome this became, which is why collaboration or outsourcing was never considered. \\u201cAfter researching various ergonomic aspects and sound acoustics, we started to see these eartips as miniature sound domes or acoustic halls, and ended up using conventional room treatment techniques to arrive at Baroque\\u2019s unique design.\\u201d Another deviation from traditional eartip design was tuning. According to Eric, most tips target bass and/or treble attenuation or enhancement, but very few focus on the midrange. \\u201cWe\\xa0couldn\\u2019t find any tips that accentuated the mids and mid lows with warmth and vibrancy, while at the same time increasing airiness and imaging like a properly treated sound room,\\u201d he recalls. So they set out to create Baroque with this tuning in mind. A wide bore enhances treble response and expands the stage for an open, airy presentation, but the shape of the dome doesn\\u2019t reduce the bass response quite as much as conventional wide bore tips. A solid silicone core also prevents the shape of the inner dome from distorting in-ear, maintaining a consistent seal and sound regardless of the IEM pairing. Tip tech Eletech developed three new trademarked technologies for Baroque: Satin\\xe9 , Flexion and Tria Satin\\xe9 . Ergonomically, the idea was to make the tips as soft as possible, removing any sense of pressure of discomfort to allow for longer listening sessions. Baroque is made from a specially-formulated blend of Japanese medical-grade silicone, ensuring maximum comfort while mitigating any skin irritation or allergy concerns. The surface is infused with a proprietary Eletech material called Satin\\xe9, which uses wavy \\u2018nano lines\\u2019 to create a tactile, satin-like texture for even better skin feel. It also makes the tips easier to clean and more resistant to accumulating dirt and debris. Flexion . One of the design goals for Baroque was a more comfortable deep-fit insertion. Normally, a deeper insertion relies on very soft silicone so as not to put too much pressure on the ear canal opening as the tip is inserted. Even so, this is where most users will feel pressure over time, as silicone tends to expand when heated. Inspired by origami, Flexion is a strip of angular ridges around the base of the tip that \\u2018folds-in\\u2019 on itself as the eartip is inserted deeper into the ear canal, thus reducing the outward pressure. The Flexion structure also helps reduce unwanted vibration, improving the accuracy of sound transmission. What\\u2019s more, the Flexion ridge closely resembles Eletech\\u2019s angular hardware theme, proving once again that form is just as important as function when it comes to design. One thing to note here : because Baroque tips are generally much softer than regular silicone tips, and because Flexion allows them to \\u2018collapse\\u2019 for a deeper fit, those users with larger ears might want to size-up if they\\u2019re not getting a perfect seal. I\\u2019ve personally found Baroque actually\\xa0needs\\xa0to be inserted deeper to create a seal, and I\\u2019ve even had to use two different sized tips for my left and right ear to get the same degree of seal in both. Tria . Last but not least, Baroque features a series of triangular cut-outs inside the topmost rim of tip that act like a series of small acoustic panels. While the shape of these cutouts is proprietary to Eletech, we\\u2019ve seen similar techniques used in the highly successful and very popular Spiral Dot tip from JVC. In both cases, the cutouts help disperse soundwaves as they come through the nozzle, removing unwanted resonances to create a smoother response and larger acoustic space. Wired and wireless Baroque was initially introduced in three sizes: small, medium and large. They were also mainly intended for regular wired IEMs, especially modern IEMs with wider nozzles. In the past few months, Eletech added three additional sizes \\u2013 medium-small, medium-large and extra-large \\u2013 but also supplemented the original design with an all-white version better-suited for true wireless (TWS) IEMs, including one made specifically for Apple AirPods. Aside from the colour, there are some other fundamental differences between the \\u2018wired\\u2019 and wireless Baroques. For starters, TWS users tend to be more active, and generally spend less time cleaning their gear on the go. As such, Eletech added a new antibacterial coating called PureShield to its TWS and AirPods range, enhancing their longevity. They also formulated the surface with different \\u2018nano cuts\\u2019 to improve grip, while retaining the Flexion strip for added comfort. Side-by-wide, the shape of the wireless versions has also changed slightly. The tips themselves are flatter, with a reduced depth of 7mm compared to the originals\\u2019 8.25mm. This helps reduce any obstructions inside TWS cases, but also makes inserting TWS IEMs \\u2013 which normally sit deeper in the ear by design \\u2013 more comfortable. The bore is also slightly smaller, at 4.45mm diameter compared to 6mm for the originals. EMIT: Product(source=None, manufacturer=None, name=u'Eletech Baroque Eartips', sku=None, ssid=u'review-eletech-baroque-eartips', description=None, category=u'Accessories', url=u'https://theheadphonelist.com/review-eletech-baroque-eartips/', properties=[], reviews="[Review(title=u'Review: Eletech Baroque Eartips', date=u'2024-06-24', product=None, ssid=u'review-eletech-baroque-eartips', url=u'https://theheadphonelist.com/review-eletech-baroque-eartips/', click_url=None, super=None, type='pro', authors=[Person(name=u'Guy Lerner', email=None, url=u'https://theheadphonelist.com/author/gler/', ssid=u'gler')], properties=[ReviewProperty(name=None, value=u'Select comparisons I have dozens of third-party tips spread across three compartmentalised boxes. That\\u2019s no exaggeration either; I have a tip fetish second to none, even though half of my IEMs are customs. Naturally I\\u2019m not going to compare Baroque to all of these tips, not even most of them. Instead, I\\u2019ve picked out a few that are either quite similar or markedly different, just so you get an idea of where they fit on the totem pole, so to speak. JVC Spiral Dot . For the longest time these were my favourite tips. Soft, relatively short, and wide bore, these great tips are both effortlessly comfortable and very even-sounding. Like Baroque, they use pits inside the tip surface to diffuse and shape the sound, smoothing over treble peaks and enhancing midrange clarity. Compared to Baroque, I find Spiral Dots a touch more diffuse; their centre image is more hazy by comparison, and highs become slightly ethereal compared to Baroque\\u2019s smooth but solid treble. Spiral Dots also have a very smooth stem, which slips them off any IEM without a tip lip, while Baroque\\u2019s stem is grippier and usually stays in place. Final Audio Type-E . Another super-soft tip, these are very different to both Spiral Dot and Baroque in that they have a much narrower bore. This greatly focuses and elevates the bass, while attenuating treble quantity. As such they\\u2019re much better suited to bass-light IEMs compared to Baroque, and are also useful for really brining on the bass boom for bassheads with IEMs like Legend X. Ironically though, they also tend to emphasise certain treble peaks, so can come off as sibilant compared to Baroque. They also muddy the midrange with some IEMs, or at best, recess the mids just enough to dull their impact. This is fine for mid-forward IEMs, but if you prefer a neutral or forward midrange, Final\\u2019s Type-E are probably not the solution. SpinFit CP 145 . These are medium-bore tips that, to my ears, are the epitome of neutral in an eartip. With some IEMs I find them just as comfortable, if not more so, than Baroque, especially with SpinFit\\u2019s clever tip-bending design that saved many a long-nozzle IEM from the rubbish heap for me. Sound-wise they\\u2019re not as rich and saturated in the mids compared to Baroque, nor as effortlessly extended up top and down low, but are overall more balanced with more IEMs in my opinion. Divinus Velvet . There are many similarities between Baroque and these relatively new tips from South Korea. Both are barrel-shaped, wider on top than the bottom. Both also claim to have a special material blend that feels as soft as velvet (or satin). That\\u2019s where the similarities end for me, though. For one, Velvets are\\xa0\\xa0soft on the outside but rather stiff overall. That makes them harder in-ear, and since my narrow ear canals inherently struggle with most barrel IEMs (other than Baroque), they don\\u2019t fit me nearly as well. Sound-wise, Velvets don\\u2019t have the same refinement as Baroque, and can actually sound too intense with some IEMs. Not my cup of tea. Azla Crystal . A favourite of many, these are my second-favourite Azlas, after the excellent but specialist Senda EarFit MAX ASMR. The reason for citing Crystal is their similarly wide bore but much harder stem and longer nozzle, which makes them less comfortable (for me) but also more contrasty and punchy. These sound great with most IEMs, especially if you love hard-hitting and super-rumbly bass, clear vocals and sharpish treble. I find Crystals turn the dials up across the board, making the overall presentation more lively and vivid, but subsequently less finessed than Baroque. Acoustune AET 07 . Another perennial favourite, this is the third-party tip I recommend most newbies buy. Like CP 145, I find these very balanced, but there\\u2019s extra finesse to their vocals, and a tighter, cleaner bass than the SpinFits too. Compared to Baroque, I find their vocal rendering isn\\u2019t quite as organic, and in fact slightly drier. Bass is also attenuated compared to Baroque, even though they\\u2019re not wide bore, and treble, while silky, is thinner than Baroque too. Still, their combination of excellent comfort, balanced sound and low price makes these must-have tips in any collection. Closing thoughts In a crowded and competitive market, and a market where you\\u2019re literally competing with free stock tips, Eletech still saw a gap, and boy did they fill it. Baroque is quintessentially Eletech \\u2013 impeccably well made, stylishly designed, full of new technology, and very different sounding to most of their competitors. As with their cables, Eletech love their midrange purity and clarity. You\\u2019re not going to find s veiled Eletech cable, and Baroque follows suit, doubling down on midrange quality and clarity in equal measure. As for the rest \\u2013 bass, treble and techs \\u2013 that\\u2019s up to you to dial in to taste with your chosen IEMs. I don\\u2019t feel Baroque detracts from any of these metrics, but they don\\u2019t specialise in them either, other than maybe stage expansion. They do offer maximum extension at both ends of the scale though, which many IEMs don\\u2019t. If you find the right pairing, and the shape suits your ear anatomy, I can\\u2019t think of many tips that top Baroque at this point in time. They\\u2019re not the cheapest by any means, but let\\u2019s face it, in this hobby, even pricey tips are not an expensive luxury. Baroque is very much a luxury most enthusiast should easily be able to afford, and in my opinion, would be poorer not for trying. Highly recommended . The ins and outs of IEM maintenance', sku=None, ssid=u'suck-it-up-the-ins-and-outs-of-iem-maintenance', description=None, category=u'Accessories', url=u'https://theheadphonelist.com/suck-it-up-the-ins-and-outs-of-iem-maintenance/', properties=[], reviews="[Review(title=u'Suck it up! The ins and outs of IEM maintenance', date=u'2024-09-25', product=None, ssid=u'suck-it-up-the-ins-and-outs-of-iem-maintenance', url=u'https://theheadphonelist.com/suck-it-up-the-ins-and-outs-of-iem-maintenance/', click_url=None, super=None, type='pro', authors=[Person(name=u'Guy Lerner', email=None, url=u'https://theheadphonelist.com/author/gler/', ssid=u'gler')], properties=[ReviewProperty(name=None, value=u'You know that odd-looking tool in the accessories box of your latest IEM? The one with the tiny brush on one end and the wiry \\u2018hook\\u2019 on the other? Believe it or not, that\\u2019s one of the most valuable accessories you\\u2019ll get, even more so than the tips or cable or case. IEMs are precision devices, and on the whole they\\u2019re built tough enough to withstand frequent use inside your hot, sweaty, waxy ears. But they also house very fragile components, like tiny drivers with hair\\u2019s breadth cones and sensitive electronics. You\\u2019ll want to look after those components, which you can\\u2019t see, just as much you look after the pretty shells, because if they fail, those shells become little more than very expensive ornaments. In this article I\\u2019ll be covering a rarely-discussed topic in the hobby: IEM maintenance. I\\u2019ll go through the basics every IEM user should be doing to keep their IEMs clean and healthy, using little more than the tools \\u2013 like the one above \\u2013 that come in the box. But I\\u2019ll also dive deeper into business of keeping your IEMs in good health for many years, using more elaborate tools designed for frequent use in harsh conditions. If you\\u2019ve ever wondered what you could and should be doing to keep your IEMs pristine, read on. Back to basics Different IEMs are made differently and so require different degrees of care. Custom IEMs with long, twisting driver tubes require a different maintenance regimen to universal IEMs with short nozzles and mesh guards, for instance. No matter which IEMs you use, however, every IEM needs some sort of basic maintenance to keep them clean, dirt-and-dust free, and to prevent any nasties from getting at their insides. Luckily all you need for basic maintenance is cheap and easy to get: a clean surface, some alcohol swabs, a lint-free cloth, and the brush-and-pick tool. Start by wiping down your IEMs with the alcohol swab. Don\\u2019t worry, this won\\u2019t harm your precious resin shells, or even your silicone ear tips \\u2013 although it\\u2019s best to check with your ear tip manufacturer if they discourage isopropyl alcohol use for cleaning. Next, dry your shells from any excess alcohol, then carefully take one shell at a time, face the nozzle downward, and gently reach into the nozzle with the pick side of the tool to loosen any dust, grit or wax particles that might be stuck inside. While this sounds simple enough, there are some things you need to watch out for, especially when using the pick tool. First, and it goes without saying, be gentle. The acrylic coating of resin IEMs might be tough, but you want to avoid any sort of force when sliding that metal pick inside the nozzle. Some IEMs, especially those made of aluminium or titanium, can also scratch easily, in which case you might want to avoid using the pick tool altogether. Another important thing to look out for is wire mesh covers, usually installed on the inside of the nozzle of many IEMs. Depending on how they\\u2019ve been installed, some covers might be very loose, and any sort of force with the pick tool could dislodge them. You also don\\u2019t want to push any dirt or wax caught on the mesh through the mesh and into the nozzles. This is why cleaning with the nozzles pointing down is important \\u2013 gravity is your friend in this instance. If it looks like your mesh screen is heavily soiled or blocked. You might want to use the softer brush side of the tool to carefully dislodge the dirt. If there\\u2019s no mesh but you still notice trails of dirt or sticky wax deeper inside the nozzle, which your pick or brush can\\u2019t reach, then you\\u2019ll want to use an alternative cleaning method \\u2013 something like the IEM vacuums I\\u2019ll discuss shortly. Whatever you do, always keep the objective of the exercise in mind: to remove the dirt, but protect the IEM . If you make it a habit to wipe and clean off any excess dirt after every use, you\\u2019ll be able to keep your IEMs looking and sounding like new for many years, and likely won\\u2019t need to resort to the heavier-handed options for deep cleaning and long-term maintenance. Of course, chances are you \\u2013 like me \\u2013 won\\u2019t remember (or even care) to clean your IEMs every time you use them, and when days become weeks and weeks become months, that\\u2019s when you\\u2019ll need more robust tools for the job. But believe it or not, there\\u2019s even more you can do to keep your IEMs clean and healthy for the long haul. If you live in hot, humid climates, or take your IEMs to the gym, or use them for performing, or use them in the shower (just kidding, but if you do, don\\u2019t !), then there\\u2019s every chance moisture will find its way inside the shells and potentially damage the internals through corrosion. The first and most obvious defense against moisture build-up is to keep your IEMs stored in a cool, dry place, inside a sealed container or case, with a bag of silica desiccants to wick any excess moisture away. This may or may not suffice, however, especially if you have multiple IEMs to look after. In that case, there\\u2019s this\\u2026 FIR Audio IEM Dryer and Dryer Pro . For the same price as an IEM Vac ($199) and IEM Vac Pro ($299) respectively, FIR Audio also sells the IEM Dryer and IEM Dryer Pro. Both of these products, which resemble clear Pelican cases, are essentially mini dehumidifiers, working with a small powered heating fan to extract any moisture from the IEMs you seal inside. The Pro version is larger, and has a battery-powered digital display on the lid showing temperature (in Fahrenheit and Celcius) and humidity percentage. Both versions are supplied with a USB-C charging cable and inline timer so you can pre-set the amount of drying (two to three hours recommended), and a USB travel charger for power. You can also use both devices with a 2A power bank if you\\u2019re travelling somewhere without a power outlet. The other big difference between the regular and Pro versions is the number of IEMs (and attached cables) they hold. The regular version holds a maximum of two IEMs and cables, while the Pro version holds up to seven. Anyone with a larger collection of IEMs, say more than five, will find the extra $100 on the Pro version to be money well spent, not just in time saved but also in the useful digital display. In my experience, the IEM Dryer Pro is exceptionally well-built, as you\\u2019d expect from a FIR Audio accessory. The case is very robust, the interior is lined with a soft rubberised coating to prevent any scratches to your expensive gear, and while I can\\u2019t vouch for how effective (or necessary) IEM drying is in practice, I\\u2019m happy to take Bogdan Belozhonko\\u2019s word for it \\u2013 he\\u2019s never let me down. Closing thoughts Cleaning and maintaining your IEMs is the least you can do to protect what for most people is a sizeable investment. If you think you\\u2019re one of those \\u2018special people\\u2019 who don\\u2019t need to maintain their IEMs, do yourself a favour and use a UV light to look inside the nozzle\\u2026 The good thing is that maintenance is really easy to do, and not too expansive \\u2013 relatively speaking \\u2013 to do it really well. The same cleaning and drying tools covered in this article are used by touring musicians, who make up the bulk of business for both 64 Audio and FIR Audio, so you can rest assured they\\u2019ll be more than good enough for casual users and enthusiasts like us. The hardest part, as with everything in life, is to find the discipline and get into a routine of regular maintenance. If you do so regularly, even just basic cleaning with the pick tool and alcohol wipes, you\\u2019ll be most of the way there to keeping your IEMs in good shape. For those heavier users, or those with wax build-up, or those living in hot and humid places, taking the next step to vac and dry your IEMs makes sense. Any maintenance is better than no maintenance, and even if you opt for cheaper cleaning products than those mentioned here, that\\u2019s better than nothing at all. If you want guaranteed, proven quality, however, I\\u2019d highly recommend buying these tools from the professionals who use them every day for a living. EMIT: Product(source=None, manufacturer=None, name=u'SongKong: smash your music files into shape', sku=None, ssid=u'songkong-smash-your-music-files-into-shape', description=None, category=u'Accessories', url=u'https://theheadphonelist.com/songkong-smash-your-music-files-into-shape/', properties=[], reviews="[Review(title=u'SongKong: smash your music files into shape', date=u'2026-02-19', product=None, ssid=u'songkong-smash-your-music-files-into-shape', url=u'https://theheadphonelist.com/songkong-smash-your-music-files-into-shape/', click_url=None, super=None, type='pro', authors=[Person(name=u'Guy Lerner', email=None, url=u'https://theheadphonelist.com/author/gler/', ssid=u'gler')], properties=[ReviewProperty(name=None, value=u'Special thanks to Paul Taylor, the mastermind behind SongKong, for generously letting us use his software unrestricted for this review. Your support is greatly appreciated. Let\\u2019s paint a picture you may know all too well. You\\u2019ve spent years amassing a digital music collection you\\u2019re genuinely proud of \\u2013 thousands of albums, carefully ripped from CDs, downloaded from legitimate sources, or lovingly transferred from ancient hard drives. You fire up Lyrion, Roon or Audirvana, pour yourself a dram of something smoky, sit back in your listening chair, and prepare to browse. And then it hits you: half your library looks like it was catalogued by someone who gave up halfway through. Blank artwork. Albums split across eight phantom entries. Tracks titled \\u2018Track 01\\u2019. Classical pieces attributed to the wrong conductor, or worse, no conductor at all. Your library, despite all those hours of effort, is a bit of a mess. This is the problem that JThink\\u2019s SongKong was built to solve \\u2013 and it solves it magnificently. SongKong is an intelligent music tagger: a piece of software that goes through your entire digital music collection, identifies every track, and enriches it with accurate, detailed metadata. That means correct artist names, album titles, track numbers, release years, genres, record labels, composer credits, artwork, BPM data, mood tags, and much more. It works on any scale \\u2013 from a few hundred albums to a library of tens of thousands of tracks \\u2013 and it does so largely without you needing to lift a finger. Think of it as hiring an obsessively meticulous librarian who never sleeps, never makes a typo, and has read every album sleeve ever printed. Except this one runs on your computer \\u2013 or your NAS, your Synology server, or even a headless Docker container. Yes, but how!? This is where SongKong gets genuinely clever. It doesn\\u2019t simply rely on existing file information (which, in a disorganised library, might be entirely absent or completely wrong). Instead, it uses multiple overlapping identification methods to work out what a file actually is. The most impressive of these is acoustic fingerprinting via AcoustID \\u2013 a technology that actually listens to the audio content of your file, generates a unique digital \\u2018fingerprint\\u2019 based on the sound itself, and compares it against a vast database. It\\u2019s a bit like Shazam, but operating on your local library rather than ambient sound. A file called \\u2018track03.mp3\\u2019 with no metadata whatsoever can still be correctly identified as John Coltrane\\u2019s \\u2018A Love Supreme, Pt. II \\u2013 Resolution\\u2019 from the original 1965 Impulse! pressing. That\\u2019s remarkable. AcoustID isn\\u2019t infrallible though, and this is where overlapping identification methods come together to root out false-positives. SongKong also uses Albunack Disc IDs \\u2013 checksums calculated from the track lengths and ordering within a folder \\u2013 which can identify a complete album almost instantaneously, without the computational overhead of fingerprinting every file. And for situations where it finds and identifies individual songs but can\\u2019t pin them to a specific album, it performs \\u2018Song Only\\u2019 matching: updating the fields it\\u2019s certain about (artist name, track title) whilst leaving album-specific fields alone rather than making things worse with a bad guess. That kind of restraint is genuinely impressive in software. The other crucial ingredient is database breadth. Many tagging tools rely solely on MusicBrainz \\u2013 a superb, community-maintained database, but one with gaps, particularly in niche genres. SongKong also queries Discogs, which is an absolute treasure trove for vinyl collectors, electronic music, obscure jazz pressings, and underground hip-hop. It also queries Bandcamp, covering the vast world of self-released and independent music that larger databases often miss entirely. The combination of these three sources means SongKong can correctly identify music (and gather metadata) that other tools simply give up on. Continued\\u2026 The Headphone List was created to help visitors select the best portable audio products for their needs by featuring the most comprehensive collection of headphone reviews published in an interactive, dynamic format. EMIT: Product(source=None, manufacturer=None, name=u'SongKong: smash your music files into shape', sku=None, ssid=u'songkong-smash-your-music-files-into-shape', description=None, category=u'Accessories', url=u'https://theheadphonelist.com/songkong-smash-your-music-files-into-shape/', properties=[], reviews="[Review(title=u'SongKong: smash your music files into shape', date=u'2026-02-19', product=None, ssid=u'songkong-smash-your-music-files-into-shape', url=u'https://theheadphonelist.com/songkong-smash-your-music-files-into-shape/', click_url=None, super=None, type='pro', authors=[Person(name=u'Guy Lerner', email=None, url=u'https://theheadphonelist.com/author/gler/', ssid=u'gler')], properties=[ReviewProperty(name=None, value=u'Do I need SongKong if I\\u2019m already using Lyrion, Roon or Audirvana? Here\\u2019s the thing that many people don\\u2019t fully appreciate until they\\u2019ve experienced it: Lyrion, Roon and Audirvana are brilliant, but they are only as good as the metadata they have to work with \\u2013 and none of them actually modifies your files. Roon has its own magnificent metadata engine. When it successfully identifies an album in its database, the experience is genuinely stunning: full artist biographies, personnel credits, album reviews, dynamic links between composers and performers, related recordings, and the kind of rich context that makes you feel like you\\u2019re holding the original gatefold vinyl. It\\u2019s like having a knowledgeable friend sitting next to you who knows everything about every record you own. But Roon\\u2019s identification process starts with your file tags. If those tags are poor, missing, or wrong, Roon will often fail to match the album to its database at all \\u2013 and you end up with a bare, unidentified entry that receives none of that beautiful enrichment. SongKong directly solves this problem. By cleaning and enriching your file tags before they reach Roon, SongKong dramatically increases Roon\\u2019s match rate. Albums that previously sat stubbornly in the \\u2018Unidentified\\u2019 pile suddenly spring to life with full artwork, credits, and editorial content. SongKong even has dedicated Roon-specific features, including a \\u2018Fix Songs for Roon\\u2019 profile and support for ROONALBUMTAG \\u2013 a tag that allows correct handling of box sets, which are notoriously tricky in Roon without this. For Lyrion and Audirvana users the relationship is equally important \\u2013 perhaps even more so, since both lean more heavily on your actual file metadata rather than supplementing it from its own database. What you tag is what you get. SongKong ensures that what you get is excellent. Bottom line: why should I care? If you\\u2019ve spent thousands on a DAC, amplifier, speakers, and a premium music player subscription, and your library is a disorganised mess of mislabelled files, you\\u2019re not getting what you paid for. The hardware and software that should be delivering you musical joy is instead spending its time struggling with bad data. SongKong is the bit of the chain that people often overlook \\u2013 the foundation that everything else rests on. It\\u2019s not glamorous in the way a new amplifier is glamorous. There are no glowing valves, no premium binding posts, no beautifully machined casework. It\\u2019s software that fixes tags. But without it, your digital music setup is like a high-end turntable with a worn stylus: technically impressive, fundamentally let down by one small but critical component. With it, your library becomes browsable, beautiful, and complete. Albums live where they should. Artwork displays correctly. Classical works are properly credited. Roon identifies everything and enriches it with the context that makes music discovery a genuine pleasure rather than a frustration. The difference, once experienced, is hard to overstate. Closing thoughts SongKong isn\\u2019t trying to be the flashiest tool in the audiophile\\u2019s cabinet. It doesn\\u2019t stream music, it doesn\\u2019t have a DSP engine, it doesn\\u2019t upscale your files to DSD512. What it does is something altogether more fundamental: it makes sure your music knows what it is, is metadata rich and easily explored. In a world where the experience of digital music is increasingly defined by software intelligence \\u2013 where the joy of discovery in Roon depends on rich, accurate data, where Audirvana\\u2019s browsing experience lives and dies by your file tags and augmented metadata\\xa0 \\u2013 SongKong has quietly become one of the most important tools a music lover can own. It\\u2019s priced fairly, it runs on almost anything, and once you\\u2019ve pointed it at a previously chaotic library and watched it transform the result, you\\u2019ll wonder how you ever managed without it. If you\\u2019re serious about your digital music, stop worrying about your next equipment upgrade for five minutes. Sort out your metadata first. SongKong makes that surprisingly painless.\\xa0Highly recommended. That kind of restraint is genuinely impressive in software. The other crucial ingredient is database breadth. Many tagging tools rely solely on MusicBrainz \\u2013 a superb, community-maintained database, but one with gaps, particularly in niche genres. SongKong also queries Discogs, which is an absolute treasure trove for vinyl collectors, electronic music, obscure jazz pressings, and underground hip-hop. It also queries Bandcamp, covering the vast world of self-released and independent music that larger databases often miss entirely. The combination of these three sources means SongKong can correctly identify music (and gather metadata) that other tools simply give up on.
