EMIT: Product(source=None, manufacturer=None, name=u'Impressions: TGXEAR Sunniva', sku=None, ssid=u'impressions-tgxear-sunniva', description=None, category=u'Earbuds', url=u'https://theheadphonelist.com/impressions-tgxear-sunniva/', properties=[], reviews="[Review(title=u'Impressions: TGXEAR Sunniva', date=u'2023-09-06', product=None, ssid=u'impressions-tgxear-sunniva', url=u'https://theheadphonelist.com/impressions-tgxear-sunniva/', click_url=None, super=None, type='pro', authors=[Person(name=u'Guy Lerner', email=None, url=u'https://theheadphonelist.com/author/gler/', ssid=u'gler')], properties=[ReviewProperty(name=None, value=u'Sound impressions Jim describes Sunniva\\u2019s tonality as \\u2018warm neutral\\u2019, and that\\u2019s basically that in a nutshell. Compared to Serratus it\\u2019s definitely warmer and less neutral, though, similar to Ripples in that regard. Bass is the standout here, and Sunniva doesn\\u2019t shy away from infusing music with a healthy dose of midbass regardless of what music you\\u2019re playing. A midbass-forward tuning is generally thicker, with more note weight, and that\\u2019s the main characteristic of Sunniva\\u2019s bass for me. It\\u2019s a faster midbass presentation than Ripples, though, which means I\\u2019m feeling more sub-bass by comparison, but I still feel Sunniva suffers from substantial sub-bass rolloff compared to Serratus \\u2013 and definitely compared to most dynamic driver IEMs. The Beryllium driver in Sunniva must be doing something right though, because the texture and detail in the midbass is impressive. It\\u2019s also reasonably fast, so you\\u2019ll probably hear less bloom than you would with the slower Ripples. Sunniva\\u2019s warmth feeds, rather than bleeds, into the midrange, with lower mids in particular benefitting the most. Male vocals are satisfyingly chesty, and instruments are played with plenty of note weight too. There is a touch of veil (or maybe it\\u2019s just the thickness that I\\u2019m not used to), in some midrange notes, with less air between elements as a result, at least when compared to buds with more incisive clarity and transparency like Serratus. Sunniva\\u2019s fuller midrange note are definitely more romantic, if a touch less accurate, than Serratus\\u2019s thinner rendering, but the step up in midrange resolution and clarity over Ripples is noticeable. Treble notes also benefit from the faster driver in Sunniva. There\\u2019s plenty of sparkle and detail here, with enough extension to satisfy all but die-hard treble fans. It\\u2019s not quite the open-sounding majesty of Serratus treble, so strings won\\u2019t soar as high or sound as delicately sweet, but once again it\\u2019s an upgrade over Ripples, especially in terms of resolution. In fact, to continue the theme, I\\u2019m going to characterise Sunniva as a more technically-accomplished take on Ripples. Its tuning is such that the fundamentals are very similar to how I hear Ripples, but the shortcomings I noted in my Ripples review \\u2013 resolution, speed and extension \\u2013 have all been solved for the most part with Sunniva. I\\u2019m also hearing a bigger stage, with better separation and imaging ability, though Sunniva still trails Serratus in these metrics, for me. That\\u2019s possibly more to do with its tuning, because Serratus\\u2019s cleaner sound makes it easier to pick out subtle elements and details in the music that are less forward in the mix with Sunniva. It\\u2019s also worth noting that TGXEAR has a bass mod that works by partially blocking the bass vents and reducing overall midbass levels by 3dB. That should go some way to shedding off some of the veil I still hear (as a result of the tuning), and also opening up some of the other frequencies. Personally I like Sunniva\\u2019s tuning as-is, and would much rather pick a bud like Serratus for a different flavour. Overall, Sunniva is a very apt name for an earbud that plays on the warmer side, but is both more advanced and refined than its \\u2018predecessor\\u2019 in all the ways that matter. Closing thoughts When I first heard about Sunniva, at the time TGXEAR\\u2019s new \\u2018flagship\\u2019, I was hoping for something more in line with Serratus, which is the bud that really transformed how I feel about earbuds in general. Instead, Sunniva turned out to be a direct upgrade of the Ripples blueprint, so for those who have and love Ripples it\\u2019s an easy rec. As such, I don\\u2019t consider Sunniva an upgrade to Serratus, and in fact, their differences make them far more complementary than competitive. I think if you love the clean, crisp and transparent Serratus sound, with its outstanding extension into both sub-bass and treble, Sunniva will give you an almost opposite tuning, only this time with technical ability to match. Sunniva would still be my pick for the likes of jazz, acoustic and rock, whereas Serratus is a better performer for classical, pop and electronic music. For not much more than the price of Ripples, Sunniva is a solid step up, and makes an ideal Ying and Yang pairing with Serratus. I\\u2019d go as far as to say Ripples has no more reason to exist now that Sunniva does. As with all of Jim\\u2019s buds, Sunniva represents outstanding value, and at $350 it\\u2019s almost impossible not to be bowled over by the sound. Suffice it to say I\\u2019m yet to hear an IEM that can do what these buds do at the same price point, so even if you\\u2019e not convinced that buds are for you, I\\u2019d at least recommend an audition. In the world of bespoke, hand-made audio gear, few come with more attention to detail and love for the craft than Jim Park\\u2019s range of class-leading earbuds. My personal journey with earbuds has been a mixed one. I\\u2019m probably not who you\\u2019d want as a poster-child for these confounding devices, always fiddling with the fit, and not really agreeable with the idea of putting foam anywhere near my ears. But on the flipside, listening to some of Jim\\u2019s creations has changed my perception of what\\u2019s possible with this format. Let me be clear: earbuds are never going to replace IEMs for me. I love my bass response too much, and bass is the one aspect of earbud sound that\\u2019s never going to come close to a good IEM, no matter what you do or how you wear it. Yes, that\\u2019s probably subjective, and I know many users who absolutely love the bass from their buds and even prefer it to the physicality of IEM bass. But just about every aspect of this hobby is subjective, so I\\u2019m willing to stick my neck out and say that if IEM bass is your thing, you might be left wanting with earbuds. This context is important because the earbud we\\u2019re looking at today, Sunniva, is at least in part a bass-first bud. Its elevated midbass tuning colours the sound beyond Jim\\u2019s usual neutral-leaning style, and even though on balance it\\u2019s still a fairly neutral-sounding IEM, you\\u2019re going to have to really love your midbass colouration to enjoy what Sunniva is about. Sadly, for me, midbass colouraton is not exactly my cup of tea, even as a bass lover. I covered some of this conundrum in my Ripples review, and while I don\\u2019t think it\\u2019s a spoiler to say that Sunniva is a big step up from Ripples, it follows a similar tuning style that may or may not jive with your preferences. That said, there will be plenty of you for whom this warmer, fuller type of tuning is just the ticket, so don\\u2019t be put off by my biases, and let\\u2019s take a closer look at Sunniva \\u2013 Jim\\u2019s \\u2018Gift of the Sun\\u2019. Package, build and specs Sunniva ships in a similar oval zipper case to earlier TGXEAR buds, but the accessory count has been upgraded. You get no less than three types of foam covers: clear, focus and smooth (although I also got a few sets of \\u2018neutral\\u2019), and Jim was kind enough to include a pack of \\u2018wingtips\\u2019 in the case too (more on this later). The design and build of the buds themselves have also been upgraded, with Sunniva sporting a \\u20187N Ohno Continuous Casting\\u2019 silver cable, terminated in a 4.4mm pentaconn plug, and featuring colourful, ceramic-like hardware that\\u2019s pleasant on the eye and not too weighty. Technically Sunniva is easier to drive than Serratus (and about the same as Ripples), with a 180ohm Beryllium PVD N52 Driver rated at 102dBs/mW. Practically speaking that\\u2019s at least 15 clicks less on the volume dial of the HiBy RS8 in high gain compared to Serratus, so while it\\u2019s easier to drive, it\\u2019s still going to demand a powerful portable amp for optimal performance, and will no doubt scale even further with desktop amplification. The driver itself can be clearly seen through the \\u2018freeflow\\u2019 faceplate design, reminiscent of mag wheels on a fancy sports car. The whole look and feel of Sunniva is top tier, and while there\\u2019s only so much you can do with the plastic housing of these buds, it\\u2019s good to see the level of the design evolving as the range matures. As for fit, well, nothing much has changed here, but I did have an epiphany trying out the included wingtips: silicone is simply far more comfortable than foam. While Jim doesn\\u2019t recommend using wingtips with MX500 shells, for my ears they make the buds much easier to wear. I still use the foam covers, cutting a small slit in each cover so it slides over the wingtip, but once it\\u2019s in place, the buds don\\u2019t move, and I feel the seal (and sound) is better too. On that note, it\\u2019s very possible that my ear anatomy just isn\\u2019t ideal for MX500 shells, but I get an even less comfortable fit with bell shells. Still, fit with the wingtips is a big improvement, so if, like me, you struggle with getting earbuds to stay in your ears and not cause pain after a short listen, give the wings a try. EMIT: Product(source=None, manufacturer=None, name=u'Kotori Audio Zephyr \u2013 Subversion', sku=None, ssid=u'kotori-audio-zephyr', description=None, category=u'Earbuds', url=u'https://theheadphonelist.com/kotori-audio-zephyr-review/', properties=[], reviews="[Review(title=u'Kotori Audio Zephyr \\u2013 Subversion', date=u'2024-11-13', product=None, ssid=u'kotori-audio-zephyr', url=u'https://theheadphonelist.com/kotori-audio-zephyr-review/', click_url=None, super=None, type='pro', authors=[Person(name=u'Kevingzw', email=None, url=u'https://theheadphonelist.com/author/kevin-goh/', ssid=u'kevin-goh')], properties=[ReviewProperty(name=None, value=u'Disclaimer: I thank Ray Tan from Kotori Audio for generously providing me with the Kotori Zephyr in exchange for an honest review . On behalf of the team at the Headphone List, we thank him for his generosity and trust in THL. TLDR: The Kotori Zephyr conscientiously objects to Harman or Harman-like tunings. The Zephyr excels in mid-forward vocal presentations, punctuated by a texturally-rich and mid-bass defined punch: a gem in the sub-$200 bracket. While the Zephyr isn\\u2019t the most macro-dynamically exciting, the Zephyr\\u2019s technical yet inoffensive presentation makes it a highly-competent set for listeners who tire of the Harman meta. Pros: + \\u2018Bauhaus\\u2019 inspired design with a bold, faceted faceplate and black-resin shells + Excellent QC, fit and finish + Like the Vampire before it, the Carbon V2 stock cable is the \\u2018best\\u2019 stock cable I\\u2019ve had the privilege of reviewing \\u2013 Supple and ultra-flexible. + Speedy, tight and taut mid-bass punch. + Mid-range clarity accentuates the trailing notes of female vocals. + Good top-end clarity and imaging depth Cons: \\u2013 Deep sub-bass roll-off lacks the satisfying girth and rumble \\u2013 weaker macro-dynamics \\u2013 Presence region experiences a (perceived) truncation in the upper-treble, lacking the characteristic shine that defines brighter instruments. \\u2013 Average lateral staging width Harman-tunings are the enduring craze of the audiophile world (and for scientifically substantive reasons). However, listening for leisure is primarily defined by \\u2018feel\\u2019. Individual preferences will take precedence over a decade-spanning aggregation of listening preferences. That\\u2019s where Kotori Audio fits into the grand narrative. Instead of religiously adhering to the Harman dogma, the Zephyr strictly follows the subjective preferences and tuning prowess of the talented ensemble of engineers behind the Kotori brand. Every release is tuned in accordance to the demographic it is meant to placate. The Vampire, for instance, embodies fatigueless and stress-free listening. The Zephyr, on the other hand, allegedly blends the technical virtuosity of the Dauntless\\u2019 (their virgin IEM release) with a tamer frequency response. The Zephyr is a commendable gamble in the face of the lingering influence the Harman-meta has across the audiophile world. Priced at $179, the Zephyr enters a crowded and contested space that is rapidly in flux. Today, I assess its inherent viability against other industry leaders. The Zephyr can be directly pre-ordered on Kotori Audio\\u2019s official website. The Zephyr integrates a dual-driver hybrid configuration, featuring an extremely-stiff and resilient TI-coated PU dynamic driver (that is quite the mouthful), alongside a Knowles balanced armature tweeter. The PBAS comprises a complex network of acoustic pathways and a carefully-placed balancer tube to mitigate the build-up of pressure within the 3D-printed chamber. The balancer tube is surgically machined and cut with a laser, precisely fabricated to ensure stringent tolerances are satisfied (in this instance, approximately 30 microns \\u2013 1/2 the width of a human hair. Significant Inter-chamber pressure relief is achieved by allowing the TI-coated dynamic driver to freely actuate with greater flexibility and momentum by eliminating the presence of \\u2018back-pressure\\u2019 situated behind the driver. Allegedly, the end result is a noticeable uptick in transient performance and low-end linearity. Similar to the Vampire before it, the Zephyr comes shipped in a small, rectangular cardboard box with an outer-sleeve. The outer-sleeve is adorned with geometric patterns and hexagonal swirls, highlighting the Zephyr\\u2019s angular-aesthetic. For a sub-$200 IEM, it\\u2019s readily apparent the Kotori Audio team labored greatly to ensure the customer enjoys a premium unboxing experience: a value-add that is often neglected by IEMs multiples in price. Under the hood, you\\u2019ll find the following accessories: Every accessory inclusion looks and feels high-quality. There is virtually nothing to critique in the Zephyr\\u2019s exceptional package. Svelte and sleek, the Zephyr\\u2019s eye-catching angular faceplate is impossible ignore. The multi-faceted, stepped machining on each faceplate draws inspiration from Bauhaus architecture, sharply characterized by flat colors and pragmatic design. In a practical setting, the stepped \\u2018topographical\\u2019 lines assists in setting and removing in one\\u2019s ears by enhancing its ease-of-grip. The remaining portion of each resin shell is 3D-printed in \\u2018piano-black\\u2019, with shiny lustrous application of lacquer. The seams between the faceplates and shells are tautly sealed, with no visible specks of excess glue. The Zephyr encapsulates both style and substance, with a distinctive appearance that stands out from the overabundance of generic acrylic clones flooding the market. The Zephyr has a fairly sizeable shell and chamber, housing its fairly modest hybrid-driver array. Because of its larger sizing, the overall shell doesn\\u2019t sit flush against the inner-ear and counter-helix. Instead, it protrudes out, albeit with snug fit. The Zephyr has a deep nozzle-length that just a few hairs away from the Etymotic\\u2019s infamous deep-insertion fit. However, it doesn\\u2019t burden the listener with complaints of soreness. Achieving a snug fit was not a problem for my ears. Each nozzle has a raised notch/lip that prevents the attached ear-tips from falling off. Long-term listening on the Zephyr proved to be a comfortable and fuss-free experience, with no noticeable points of discomfort during my prolonged testing sessions. The Zephyr has no visible pin-hole sized vents that I could locate on the shell. If I had to make an educated guess, the Zephyr must adhere to a similar philosophy as its younger sibling, the Dauntless, which purposefully eliminates the existence of a vent to \\u2018choke\\u2019 the driver into fluttering rapidly in-place, with each extrusion of the diaphragm providing an energetic and resolving sound. In practice, this greatly strengthens the Zephyr\\u2019s ability to block out extraneous ambient noise in urban environments. There\\u2019s not much for me to say that hasn\\u2019t been said. The Carbon V3 cable (thus far), is still the undisputed champion of IEM cables in terms of extreme flexibility, suppleness and malleability. The cable is gentle to the touch, with almost no resistance or proneness to memory. Bend it any way, shape or form, and it contours to your body. Walking around with the Carbon V3s is an effortless and unobtrusive endeavor. The custom-hardware adorning the Y-split and 3.5mm unbalanced terminations is beautifully satinised and emblazoned with the Kotori Audio branding. There\\u2019s virtually nothing I can critique, save for the lack for swappable terminations. However, when it comes to the meat-and-bones of the IEM cable, the Carbon V3 ticks all the right boxes and then some. The mids are clear, and while the highs may lack a bit of sparkle, they remain crisp enough for most casual listening. Don\\u2019t expect crazy, kilo-buck level performance. This is an earbud for the masses. Bass is where the EarFun Clip shows its limitations. As with most open-ear designs, the bass response is not very prominent. Sub-bass frequencies lack weight and impact, making bass-heavy genres like Hip-Hop or electronic music feel underwhelming. However, the bass is present enough to enjoy warmer genres like acoustic or jazz, especially with a slight bass boost from the custom EQ. It\\u2019s not the most powerful low end, but it\\u2019s serviceable for more mellow genres. The midrange is adequate for a budget open-ear earbud, though it\\u2019s not particularly detailed. Instruments feel slightly boxed in, but the transient response is lively, giving each note a bit of punch and velocity. This makes it ideal for instrumental genres where fast note attacks are key. However, vocals don\\u2019t sound all that clear. They tend to sound recessed and underpowered, especially in busier tracks where they can struggle to cut through the mix. The Clip isn\\u2019t ideal for vocal-heavy music, as the mids lack the clarity needed to give voices full presence. The treble is the standout feature of the EarFun Clip. The high frequencies are crisp, clear, and detailed, adding a sense of sparkle and airiness to the sound. There\\u2019s a delicate tone to the highs that\\u2019s highly satisfying, without crossing into harsh or bright territory. The clarity and articulation of the treble give the Clip a refreshing quality, especially for instrumental music where high-frequency retain a commendable level of cleanliness. The EarFun Clip delivers a surprisingly wide soundstage for an open-ear design. The sound feels airy and expansive, with distinct spatial imaging that allows individual elements in the mix to occupy specific positions. The sound floats in a non-linear way, which can make it tricky to localize certain sounds, but the stereo field still feels well-structured. For an open-ear design, this kind of depth is impressive. Enabling Theater Mode expands the soundstage even further, providing better left-right channel separation. Without it, the soundstage feels more compact but retains that open-air, spacious quality. The Clip\\u2019s soundstage is one of its strongest points. The EarFun Clip delivers a unique listening experience with its under-ear design and impressive soundstage. While it may not deliver thumping bass or crystal-clear vocals, it excels in delivering immersive spatial audio, solid treble, and a comfortable fit for those who don\\u2019t want to stuff earbuds into their ears. For casual listeners, runners, or anyone looking for secure and comfortable open-ear earbuds, the Clip is an excellent choice. With Bluetooth 6.0, LDAC support, and an intuitive companion app, the Clip packs a lot of features for under $100, making it a solid offering in the open-ear category. It does a lot of things I love and no other IEM is as technically, dynamically capable as this, under $1000.', type='conclusion'), ReviewProperty(name=None, value=u'Hailing from Poland, Europe, Custom Art has been making some of the best IEMs in various price ranges for a while. They have had the legendary Harmony 8 series, a flagship IEM loved by the connoisseurs and desired by consumers. The iconic Fibae series gave us some of the best mid range IEMs like Fibae 3 and 4. Known for its technical capabilities, we still have the flagship Fibae 7 ultimate. With a mixed feelings in my heart, it seems like this iconic European brand is changing it\\u2019s strategy. They do not have the most of the Fibae\\u2019s BA only lineup anymore. The 3\\xd73 was the last only BA IEM that was available for about an year before getting discontinued this June. Thankfully the newer generation of IEMs are highly capable. Equipped with hybrid drivers they are coming for the mainstream consumers. The first one of this lineup was the Fibae 5. An IEM that isn\\u2019t a flagship but can do everything without any hiccups. Exhibiting excellent details and resolution it takes the legacy of the Fibae lineup ahead. Even though it has hybrid drivers in it\\u2019s heart the first one labelled as hybrid by Custom Art is the new H3 pro. This has a simpler set of drivers but knowing what Custom Art is capable of I have my hopes high. Priced at \\u20ac700 or $700 for Universal and \\u20ac800/$800 for the custom fit version H3 pro houses an interesting 3 drivers setup. Two DD drivers are responsible for the Lower and mid range while a single planar driver takes care of the highs. A setup that has a lot of promise as we all know DD drivers are known for their bass capabilities and planar drivers are loved for their treble extension. Get one from these links: UNBOXING AND ACCESSORIES: No surprises here. We get an exactly same set of accessories as the Fibae 5. Same box, same packing material, same tips and carry case. What\\u2019s different is the cable. Hybrid 3 pro ships with a Null Audio Ethos cable which isn\\u2019t as good looking as the Fibae 5 cable. I will suggest you guys to opt for the 8w upgrade cable. It\\u2019s asking price of $85 is totally worth it. CABLE: If we look into the details, we get a Null Audio Ethos cable which has 4 cores of\\xa05N copper\\xa0wires. This black cable looks decent but aesthetically doesn\\u2019t live up to the price range. We get much more eye catching default cables with IEMs at a much lower price range. The good thing is the low profile which is much easier to handle and easier to carry around. It isn\\u2019t bouncy and feels reasonably supple with fairly low microphonics. It does have a bit of memory but is easily manageable. The cable guides doesn\\u2019t exert any pressure or induce any discomfort and along the 3.5mm jack, cable slider and Y-splitter are minimal in size. I definitely would have liked a cable similar to the Fibae 5 but one is supposed to upgrade it anyway so\\u2026 its okay I guess. BUILD QUALITY: At first glance, it looks exactly same as the Fibae 5. I used to love the filled in shells of the Fibae 3 and 4 but thanks to the DD and moving parts these new gen Custom Art IEMs are shipping with an airy shell. This resin shell is as strong as anything else available in the market but it might not survive a fall of more than 3 feet on a hard surface. The shape, design and ergonomics are very similar to the 3 and 4 but are bigger. It is not as ergonomically designed but the fit is fantastic (if fit is a concern get the Custom version). The boxy shell design with the nozzle flowing out of the shell might feel a bit odd to some but once placed inside, this IEM has a very good fit thanks the curvy edges and small size. The nozzle too is not big and doesn\\u2019t feel odd when used with foam or small tips. It has slightly shorter nozzle than the Fibae 5 and is more stable inside my ears. There are no vents but a port that can be partially closed with the acoustic filter. Let\\u2019s talk about durability. Will this stand a drop from 3 feet on the floor? It can, Custom art is known for their build quality, the IEM feels solid and sturdy in the hand but try not to drop your IEMs, just to be safe. PAIRING WITH CABLES: EFFECT Audio Cadmus 8w: Out of the Signature Series This is the cable of choice for me. It has the balance and details and the slightly mid favoring signature is a delight especially for those who like vocal tracks. The improvements in stage are appreciated as always. Oh, Don\\u2019t worry, H3 pro is not V shaped but this cable definitely gives it different dynamics to work with and this IEM pulls it off effortlessly. It easily moulds into a mid forward IEM with tighter lows with slightly less energetic highs. It doesn\\u2019t lose any of its dynamics, sonic abilities or crispiness. To my delight, notes in the mid range, especially the vocals have taller notes and are a bit more emotional and full. Notes across the spectrum have excellent finishing definition and clarity. Thanks to faster decay we get w lot of air in between notes. Treble has similar lively feel and extension remains the same but are slightly less forward. Is this the cable for this IEM? Yes and no. Yes, if you want a mid forward sound and no if you want a more musical outcome. Let\\u2019s move on to the Ares S then. EFFECT AUDIO Ares S 8w: This is a proper copper cable with thicker and fuller notes. The 4 core version is more balanced and doesn\\u2019t help the lows as much as we expect from copper cables. If you want your IEM to respond to cables and make it warm, thick and organic, this is the cable to get under $500. This is a cable that can make even the sharpest IEMs lose their attack and that\\u2019s what it does with the H3 pro. Lows have more weight, subs are more or less same but the mid bass is fuller and punchier. Mid range notes and vocals gain a lot of body and texture. It still retains good amount of finishing definition and clarity. Treble is reasonably sharp but does lose the extra bit of sharpness and energy in the upper treble range. The improvements in stage are much better than the stock cable but the fuller note doesn\\u2019t leave a lot of air in between. Is this the best cable? Same, yes and no. If you want warmth and smoother notes, this is the perfect cable but if you want to retain the bite and sharpness and can do with slightly lesser bass body then opt for the Cadmus 8w. Custom Art Upgraded 8w SPC : To be honest, this IEM is meant to be paired with SPC cables. It\\u2019s dynamic drivers like the way SPC and hybrid cables deliver the power. Is this different from the stock cable? Signature wise, no. All we get is a bigger stage and filling notes. Attack, presentation, sonics, tonality and timber remains the same. Is this upgrade worth it? Oh definitely. If you like the stock sound, this is one excellent cable to invest in. PW Audio 1960s SE: Can I take this IEM to the Flagship levels? Hell yeah!! The potential of this cable takes this sub$1000 to the level of Flagships like Noble Khan. Khan is a bit more aggressive, this pairing is forgiving. We get excellent control and some of the best dynamics. Subs have better presence, notes have superb texture with pleasing tonality while the definition is sky high. Increase in stage dimensions is nearly doubled and this added space provides excellent cue placement and air even when notes now are more full bodied. Treble is relaxed yet agile and lively. The most impressive part is the synergy and cohesion this cable brings into the equation. I have heard analytical cables with huge stages but hadn\\u2019t heard a cable this much capable in synergy and cohesion. It\\u2019s transient effects and the way notes are formed is captivating. PAIRING WITH SOURCES: I have used a handful of Custom Art IEMs and even when they were BA based IEMs, I never faced any issues when it comes to source matching. Thanks to the Flat impedance technology these IEMs are not demanding when it comes to sources. Most of them will sound very good with any source. Same is true for H3 pro too. It has an impedance of 7ohm and sensitivity of 110db which is plenty loud. I have paired it with weak sources like my Mobile phone and powerful sources like Micro Signature and the response from this IEM is reasonably uniform. There are no compressed or jarred feelings even with the Infinix GT10 pro. Yes, the qualities of the source do reflect on the IEM, with Micro Signature it sounds open and slightly fuller with pleasing tonality and timber. When paired with a dongle like Fiio KA5 we get excellent balance and details with slightly tighter notes. In conclusion I can say that one can pair this beautiful IEM with mostly anything but yes, it\\u2019s an expensive IEM and do it a favor and get at a decent dongle for it and it will not disappoint you. SOUND QUALITY: Custom Art IEMs are known for their excellent technical capabilities and the H3 pro stays true to this. It has the similar technicalities of the Fibae 5. The DD drivers are tuned to deliver excellent control and precision while the planar driver delivers excellent resolution and clarity. Tonality and timber are very much neutral while the overall decay speed is a bit on the faster side delivering crispier notes. There is a hint of warmth with right amount of sharpness. It\\u2019s soothing while hitting the right spots. I am using a generic wide bore tips and the stock cable, Fiio KA5 and Shanling UA5 as source. BASS: I have been looking for this. Sheer perfection. It not only has excellent control and synergy it has the best balance of speed, body and weight. This balance gives it an unparallel dynamic range which harbours one of most capable and versatile range of notes under its belt and the amount of effortlessness is simply mind boggling. This type of range is something only flagships are able to pull off. Is it a bassy IEM? No, not really upto the bass head levels but when it hits, its mouth watering. Stupendous performance. What surprises me is the dynamics. It\\u2019s surprisingly outstanding. Its accuracy is captivating. Let me explain. When an upper bass note hits with after decay residue what we usually get are softer note that land with a cushioned feel and the hardness at the finishing region is comprised. H3 pro aces this with superior control. The hardness is intact while the energy of the decaying note drops rapidly yet stays on for a small while, exhibiting excellent weight, cohesion and control. Subs and mid range notes have similar feel. The actual notes have excellent transparency and energy while the decay starts at a lower energy but stays for a while, bestowing the H3 pro with mud free yet full bodied notes. Try finding an IEM this capable under $1000. I will wait. Sub bass extension is superior to its counter parts and the rumble is very good too. Mid bass is fuller with a dynamic area of impact. The way this IEM adapts to the need of the tracks is simply mind blowing. What an IEM. What a low end. MID RANGE: I love the mid range. And the clarity we get here is superb but this is the weaker side of this IEM and it eats IEMs like Monarch mk2 and 64audio Duo for lunch. Just imagine where it stands. Then why do I think it is its weaker point? It\\u2019s the presentation. It changes with the filters but one has to change filters and one filter will satisfy only one set of listeners. Let\\u2019s discuss the scenarios first. The stock brown filter has excellent clarity, Superior finishing definition and energy. Its crispier notes deliver excellent resolution and traction. But while it\\u2019s a hit for those who like details and clarity, for the other half it can be a thin, dry and not so smooth sounding average performer. For those seeking body and fuller notes can switch to the stock narrow bore tips or Spinfit CP100. It brings in juicier notes and reduces the sharpness a bit, but if you have to trounce the sharpness, switch to the yellow filters. It takes the energy level down a bit and pushes the IEM into a more pronounced W shaped presentation. A detail seeker (like me) will feel dissatisfied with this. So here are the scenarios. 1. Brown filter: Fantastic details, Crips and more balanced but thin and dry for the lover of smoothness. 2. Yellow filter: Juicier and solid but lack the extra bite that some of us enjoy. I love details and clarity and the Brown filter is brimming with these so here\\u2019s a more detailed analysis with this fantastic filter. Vocals have a bit more pop and sound superb with excellent texture and precision. Notes have the right amount body, both thin and thick, depending upon the track. The decay is paced superbly. I am in awe the way this IEM nails male vocals. It brings sharpness with artists like \\u201cThe Weeknd\\u201d and has the required throatier body with \\u201cDua Lipa\\u201d. With \\u201cPapa Roach\\u201d we get the middle ground, not sharp, not thin, sheer accuracy. I don\\u2019t always run out of superlatives but this IEM has so much going for it that it has made me lose words. Instruments around the vocals are slightly less energetic to be less on the face. They still have superb accuracy and clarity. It has the bite but it\\u2019s the playful type that leaves an impression that makes us come for more but doesn\\u2019t leave a negative impression. Air between instruments is superb too. There is no additional or excess energy in the upper treble region and it blends nicely into the lower treble. Love this tuning, perfect for those who like technicalities without being sharp or thin, dynamics without being edgy or dull. HIGHS: After delivering an impressive mid range we are treated with an excellent treble performance. Powered by a planar driver, this treble region is tuned around the Fibae 5 and performs exceptionally. Keep in mind that I didn\\u2019t get any chance to work on Fibae 7 while the Fibae 4 is very good, it doesn\\u2019t have the endless type of extension we get with the Fibae 3 and I am happy to report that we are being treated with the Endless type treble extension here, without compromising details or transparency. There is barely any drop in the upper treble region but the slightly less energetic notes are there to make this IEM comfortable for longer listening sessions. What we do get is enjoyable spark and lively presentation, superb transparency, resolution and definition. Coming from balanced upper mids there are no demons in the lower treble region. In fact the whole treble region is devoid of any unnecessary, uncomfortable sharpness or peaks. Layering and separation are excellent with very good contrast between back and foreground acts across the spectrum. We get some of the best air between instruments. IMAGING AND STAGE: Hybrid 3 pro has a well rounded stage. It has above average height, one of the best X-axis width and decent Z-axis depth but the stage has excellent presence both behind the head and superb depth below the ears. It has a well rounded stage distribution and no area feels busier or cramped. It places most of the instruments and some of the vocals out of the head while bass and rest of the vocals are placed inside the head. Imaging is excellent with top notch cue placement and plenty of air between instruments. Thanks to Planar driver, sonics are much better than what we get with Monarch mk2. There is no boxy or restrained feeling to worry about either. TUNING FILTERS : Without the filters this IEM feels hollow. It still has the same kind of details, texture and resolution but the after decay effects are more prolonged and are the notes have compromised bases. They are way too wide at the floor and feel hollow. Bass is flattish while the mids and vocals are nearly similar (with energy and emphasis) the treble is a a bit sharper with a bit of extra finishing energy. So that\\u2019s the first question answered. The Grey filter increases the bass quantity by a small margin. It provide a bit more mid bass body and considerably more rumble while\\xa0 the sub-bass gains tiny bit of volume. The area of impact is bigger, so is the punch. One good thing is the notes fire up instead of moving horizontally, leaving room for cleaner air between other instruments. We get more overall coloration too as the signature is\\xa0 more W shaped where the instruments along with the vocals are pushed back as the bass takes the driving seat. Vocals still have similar pop to them but the notes are not as tall as the mid treble or mid bass. They are juicier and fuller though. Treble plays the supporting role with similar energy and attack as the brown filter but it feels elevated because of the less energetic mid range. Sonics are slightly less prominent and transparency of micro details are slightly lowered too. The yellow filters are more balanced and delivering more precision and accuracy by reducing the bass quantity and providing more energy to the mid range. We get much tighter and lighter bass notes, they are there but aren\\u2019t heavy or impactful. It definitely cuts out any mud or loosened notes. So is this filter good for detail hogs? Kind of, mostly because it definitely has superior transparency and clarity but on the down side we get some extra sharpness that can make the whole experience uncomfortable, especially with rock or metal tracks. I definitely would like to have a bit less attack and no prizes for guessing, I am back to the brown filter for this review. It\\u2019s excellent to have options but these filters need a bit more work, putting these in isn\\u2019t a child\\u2019s play either and they need a bit of refinement before they reach the sonic levels of Apex Modules by 64audio. The treble especially is a bit off with both the filters COMPARISON: Really? What competition!! None, you are looking at the wrong segment. Comparison against IEMs under $1000 is silly. This IEM has a stupid amount of superiority over them. Its technical superiority is silly. All the competition it has is against IEMs priced over $1000. The next IEM that can match up to it is it\\u2019s elder brother Fibae 5 and after that only Effect Audio Gaea is capable enough to match up. Monarch mk2 stands nowhere close this beauty. Even Gaea bows out to the level of lower end dynamic range of this. Gaea is no competition for sub-ass presence and is hotter with sharper treble range that gets a bit uncomfortable with longer sessions. Stage is similar and sonics actually are better with the H3 pro. Vocals too are better on the Hybrid 3 pro. H3 pro is a stupidly capable IEM. It has no competition my friends, none stands against it in its price range. If you love technicalities and want to experience one of the best dynamic range and the best details under $1000, this is the IEM to get. And ohh.. Don\\u2019t worry. I will put it against its big brother, Fibae 5. Let\\u2019s dance!! VS Fibae 5: Fibae 5 is a precision tool in its own. It easily surpasses its asking price and indulges with flagships with a better cable. Its planar highs are kind of similar while the BA mids has a different type of presentation. The DD lows are a bit fuller and needs no filters to sound good. Please have a watch:', type='excerpt')], grades=[], subtests=[])]", prices='[]')
EMIT: Product(source=None, manufacturer=None, name=u'Bellos Audio X4: Goldilocks', sku=None, ssid=u'bellos-audio-x4-goldilocks', description=None, category=u'Custom In-Ear Monitor / CIEMs', url=u'https://theheadphonelist.com/bellos-audio-x4-goldilocks/', properties=[], reviews="[Review(title=u'Bellos Audio X4: Goldilocks', date=u'2024-03-20', product=None, ssid=u'bellos-audio-x4-goldilocks', url=u'https://theheadphonelist.com/bellos-audio-x4-goldilocks/', click_url=None, super=None, type='pro', authors=[Person(name=u'Guy Lerner', email=None, url=u'https://theheadphonelist.com/author/gler/', ssid=u'gler')], properties=[ReviewProperty(name=None, value=u'Thanks to Vlad and the team at Bellos Audio for sending us a sample X4 for this review. We appreciate your continued support.', type='summary'), ReviewProperty(name=None, value=u'My introduction to the Belonozkho brothers, Vlad and Bogdan, was relatively recent, with the privilege of reviewing the widely-admired Xenon 6, a product of their FIR Audio brand, just over a year ago. What I didn\\u2019t know was that, in parallel, the brothers had also started a second, smaller company, Bellos Audio , focused solely on finding new and better ways to create high-performance custom in-ear monitors for stage performers and engineers, without the usual complexity, lead times, and crazy sticker prices. The result is the X-series, a family of IEMs that boasts several \\u2018firsts\\u2019 in terms of technology and design. But what I initially dismissed as strictly pro-market products turns out is much more than that; especially the subject of this review, the series flagship X4. There\\u2019s no question the blueprint for X4 \\u2013 and the other X-series IEMs from what I gather \\u2013 was motivated by stage and mixing room requirements: robustness, easy maintenance, streamlined production, custom fit. But, in the case of X4 at least, the tuning of this IEM is what made me stop and take notice. In fact, everything else I just mentioned is secondary to what I now consider to be one of the most pleasantly tuned and technically excellent and exceptionally comfortable IEMs I\\u2019ve heard in a while, especially at this price tier. Introducing X4 Sporting a four-driver, four-way crossover configuration, the Bellos Audio X4 is a hybrid monitor in more ways that one. It uses a new 6mm dynamic driver for bass, twin balanced armature drivers for lows through highs, and a super tweeter balanced armature driver for ultra-highs. If you look closely at the nozzle opening, you\\u2019ll see the small, silver tweeter peeking through, and while this puts it very close to your ear, I\\u2019m told there\\u2019s little to no risk of wax and debris affecting the tweeter since the IEM itself doesn\\u2019t reach deep enough into the ear cavity. Still, I suggest X4 owners be fastidious when it comes to keeping the nozzle opening clean with the included brush tool. X4 features one of the more unique innovations I\\u2019ve seen in IEM design so far: a hybrid custom/universal fit, based on a 3D-printed custom shell with universal-like silicone eartips in place of the usual extended nozzle. Called In-Air Canals (these in-\\u2018ear\\u2019 nozzles don\\u2019t actually go into your ears, hence \\u2018air canals\\u2019), it\\u2019s a counterintuitive design choice that works surprisingly well. So well, in fact, that I wonder why no-one\\u2019s ever thought of it before. The basic design of custom in-ears hasn\\u2019t really changed that much over the years, dominated by resin shells and their long, snakelike nozzles perfectly matched to the inside of our ears.\\xa0It\\u2019s a solid design too, isolating stage performers \\u2013 for whom custom in-ears were first devised \\u2013 from external noise, and getting the sound delivered as close to their eardrums as possible. X4 doesn\\u2019t do away with this concept entirely, but tweaks it to very good effect. One of the downsides of long custom nozzles is the pressure they can often exert on the inside ear canals, which change size and shape when performers, especially vocalists, open their mouths to sing or talk on stage.\\xa0This can make them moderately uncomfortable to use for extended periods, but even if comfort isn\\u2019t compromised, the sound can be. That\\u2019s because the change in canal shape can also break the seal, resulting in loss of bass, which throws off feedback accuracy for the performer. In-Air Canals eliminate the twin issues of in-use discomfort and bass loss, replacing the physical nozzle with a flexible silicone tip that not only seals at the canal entrance, but also keeps the seal intact, regardless of jaw movement. But it\\u2019s the unintended consequences of this cleverly unobtrusive tweak that make X4 a game-changer, in my opinion, even more so for audiophile enthusiasts. First, this has to be one of, if not the most comfortable IEMs I\\u2019ve had the pleasure of using for longer sessions. The combination of the custom outer-ear mould with the ultrasoft, springy, shallow-fit tip, makes it literally disappear in my ears for hours of pain-free, adjustment-free use. Second, since the custom part of the shell provides part of the seal on its own, you just need to pick one of three different-sized tips to complete the seal, with minimal pressure on your inner ears. Isolation might not be quite as good as a physical resin nozzle, but the seal is almost impossible to break, at least in my experience. Third, and probably most importantly, because there\\u2019s no nozzle blocking your ear canal, there\\u2019s a much larger air volume inside your ears for the sound to travel through and resonate. You\\u2019d think this might adversely affect sound quality, but on the contrary, I feel it improves it , especially in unison with how Bellos tuned the X4. I\\u2019ll cover this in more detail in the sound impressions section, but to me the X4 sound is more open, airy and natural, but also more relaxed. Mind you, as enjoyable as this for my preferences, it might not be as agreeable for everyone, especially if you\\u2019re after a more lively, intense or tight-sounding IEM. But I digress. X4, with its In-Ear Canals, has at the very least changed my opinion of traditional custom IEMs, ostensibly for the better. Time will tell if the concept catches on and becomes more widespread beyond this small Nashville-based company and its debut monitors. Packaging, design and fit Practical is how I\\u2019d describe X4\\u2019s packaging and accessory set. This is not a luxury presentation aimed at audiophile collectors; it\\u2019s a neat, attractive, compact package that\\u2019s a step above the cardboard-box-and-nothing-else approach of some pro gear, but not quite up there with the fancy all-frills decorum of consumer-oriented IEMs. What you get is a small box with a slide-out, silkscreened sleeve, containing a solid metal puck case with the IEMs, cable, replacement tips and cleaning tool inside. The case has your name (or whatever you asked for at checkout) etched onto the lid, a very nice touch, and something I\\u2019ve only previously experienced with FIR\\u2019s flagship customs. X4\\u2019s stock cable is an 8-wire \\u2018Scorpion\\u2019 cable that\\u2019s supplied with some FIR IEMs (mine even has the FIR Audio branding), which is to say it\\u2019s a very respectable cable with a clean, clear sound profile. Although X4 uses the hardier RCX connector as standard, you can ask for a 2-pin connector instead, which makes aftermarket cable rolling much easier. As a bonus, you get the same Rigid 2-pin connector, rated for 1,000 connections, used in FIR\\u2019s Frontier Series IEMs, so there\\u2019s no compromise in quality. The three In-Air Canal tips supplied are compatible with Samsung\\u2019s Galaxy Buds 2/Pro TWS IEMs, so replacing them or getting spares is cheap and easy. It also means you can use aftermarket tips designed for the Samsung Buds, like Sedna\\u2019s AZLA Max, although I haven\\u2019t tried these myself to see how they fit and sound. Design and fit is where this IEM really stands out for me, however. The X4 shells are precision-3D printed using your custom ear moulds (or digital STL files in my case), and finished in a textured matte buff with a very pleasant skin feel. Keep in mind the matte finish isn\\u2019t quite as smooth and polished as that of the FIR customs, but has a more rustic \\u2018hammered\\u2019 look instead. X4 is available in three different colours (black, grey and army green), each with the silver Bellos Audio butterfly logo, so there\\u2019s less choice paralysis when it comes to picking one you like, at least compared to choosing from a gallery of custom faceplates and finishes. Speaking of faceplates, the X4 faceplates are attached with three countersunk steel screws, making them easy to remove for quick maintenance. Ease of serviceability is one of the design goals of the X-series, which makes sense for stage performers on the go, but also has practical benefits for audiophiles wanting a hardy, robust IEM that can take the punishment of everyday use in any location. Even the driver stack is pre-assembled and inserted into the housing before the QC phase, which makes any issues easily and consistently repairable over time. How often have you heard of IEM\\u2019s that \\u2018go bad\\u2019 and then take weeks if not months to fix. Not so with X4, which is quick swap-out. The robustness of the design makes me think these will take quite some beating before they fail, if they ever fail, which makes them even more useful as an everyday indoor/outdoor daily carry for me. Last but not least, comfort. I\\u2019ve already mentioned how comfortable I find these IEMs, so I won\\u2019t labour on that for too long here. Only to say that, once you\\u2019ve tried out a set of In-Air Canal customs, you might find your other IEMs suddenly fitting less than optimally, and missing that utterly unobtrusive feeling you get with these. Comfort extends to listening too, with a built-in Atom module effectively equalising in-ear pressure, which relieves ear fatigue for long listening sessions. The Atom module is rated at -17dB isolation (equivalent to FIR\\u2019s Gold Atom module), which trades sealed custom-like isolation for improved comfort. We appreciate your continued support.', type='summary'), ReviewProperty(name=None, value=u'Select comparisons Custom Art\\xa0FIBAE 5 ($999, reviewed here ). A neutral-tuned hybrid monitor hovering near the kilobuck mark, its only similarity with X4 is the neutral midrange tuning, but even then its note weight is on the thinner side compared to X4\\u2019s natural tone. Both its bass (too close to neutral for my liking) and treble (too far from neutral to my liking) make FIBAE 5 both livelier and more coloured than X4, better suited to genres like classical music, while lacking the punch and dynamism of X4 with modern pop, indie and rock. Campfire Audio Bonneville ($1,399, reviewed here ). More bombastic than X4, with a powerful bass presentation that leads the way, and a treble that\\u2019s never shy to let you know it\\u2019s there.\\xa0Despite having an extra BA and larger dynamic driver, Bonneville is technically close to X4. It\\u2019s got a deeper stage, sounding more holographic as a result, but all other technical metrics like resolution, imaging and separation are mostly on par. Like X4, Bonneville is an excellent all-rounder, though its tuning might still be too adventurous for some. FIR Audio Xenon 6 ($3,899, reviewed here ) and Radon 6 ($3,299, reviewed here ). For the past few weeks, I\\u2019ve been listening to all three of these wonderful IEMs, and I swear if Xe6 and Rn6 had a baby, X4 would probably be it. X4 is still a youngling, by comparison, not quite reaching the same technical heights as the FIRs, sounding less refined and assured than its older, bolder cousins (as you\\u2019d expect for less than a third of the price). From a tuning perspective, however, X4 shares traits from both. It has some of Xe6\\u2019s mid bass warmth and lower midrange fullness (not quite as much, but it\\u2019s there), combined with Rn6\\u2019s bass tautness, if not quite the same sub-bass extension. There\\u2019s no Kinetic Bass, but the tone, timbre and punch of X4\\u2019s bass are all there.\\xa0It also shares the fuller note weight of the Frontiers, closer to Xe6, and some of Xe6\\u2019s treble sparkle \\u2013 though nowhere near the same amount of detail. On the flipside, it\\u2019s gentler than Rn6\\u2019s occasionally strident treble, so makes for easier listening with certain albums. Cable pairings The one advantage of ordering X4 with a 2-pin connector is the ability to swap out the stock cable for third party alternatives. While there\\u2019s nothing inherently wrong with the stock cable, I found X4 to improve in certain areas with different cables. Eletech Raphael ($499, reviewed here ). I hear better refinement in the midrange, although the crispness from the treble \\u2013 which is typically always well extended with Eletech cables \\u2013 is still there. There\\u2019s also more resolution and texture in the bass, with a slight emphasis on midbass tilting the balance further from the sub-bass than it is with stock. Overall, Raphael is my favourite price-appropriate cable upgrade for X4, since it only costs half the price of X4 itself and so won\\u2019t break the bank. PW Audio The 1950s ($2,199). You can buy two X4\\u2019s for the price of a single The 1950s cable, but then I\\u2019d argue that the performance improvement doesn\\u2019t make it as crazy an idea as it might seem on paper. While it\\u2019s no longer PW Audio\\u2019s flagship copper cable, The 1950s hasn\\u2019t lost any of its charm \\u2013 or muscle. Bass is given a delicious quality boost, and the midrange is cleaned up, with a darker background adding to the sense of clarity. Imaging and staging is also improved, and treble is given a slight lift, adding to the sparkle and also infusing some extra air in the mix. Cable swaps are not always widely appreciated, but X4 is quite the chameleon if you\\u2019re willing to experiment and have the means to do so. The biggest benefit, for me, is more refinement, especially in the mids, though as you\\u2019ve seen above, you don\\u2019t need the most expensive cable to make that happen. Closing thoughts To be perfectly honest, I wasn\\u2019t expecting to review Bellos Audio\\u2019s X4, but now that I have, I\\u2019m so glad I did. This is an IEM that\\u2019s supposedly not made for people like me, but rather for stage and studio professionals who specifically seek out and value the many refinements in fit and robustness it gives them. It\\u2019s easy to see, then, why the X4 is catching on as one of the most versatile easy listening all-rounders money can buy right now. Not only is it relatively affordable, considering the inflated prices you\\u2019ll pay for IEMs with similar performance, but the tuning is just so good, it makes the simple joy of listening to music about as inviting as that sounds. This shouldn\\u2019t come as a surprise; after all, I\\u2019ve heard through the grapevine that the Bellos brothers know a thing or two about tuning IEMs. In all seriousness, though, X4 is actually quite different to their normal fare, even though it shares some of its stablemates\\u2019 personality traits.\\xa0While FIR\\u2019s Frontier Series is all about taking tuning risks and reaching new technical heights, Bellos Audio\\u2019s X4 plays it safe, with a sweet, relaxed tuning that\\u2019s I\\u2019d wager is almost impossible not to like. Bellos Audio was created to find new solutions to old problems for stage and studio IEMs, and the X4 is a direct result of this project. From In-Ear Canals \\u2013 which, if you ask me, is the single biggest breakthrough in custom IEM design in recent years \\u2013 to 3D-printed shells with pre-assembled drivers, this is an IEM that breaks new ground, even before we start talking sound. It\\u2019s not perfect, and on balance it still lags behind the very best IEMs I\\u2019ve heard when it comes to sheer technical performance, but it almost doesn\\u2019t matter when X4, like Goldilocks, just lends itself to lazily kicking back and enjoying track after track of naturally sweet music for hours on end. I can\\u2019t speak for performing artists and audio engineers, but for me, as an audiophile and avid enthusiast, X4 has earned its place among far more illustrious company. Highly recommended . Order your own X4 \\u2013 or try out one of the other X-series IEMs \\u2013 direct from Bellos Audio . The review is as follows. Bellos Audio is an up-and-coming in-ear\\xa0manufacturer from Nashville, Tennessee. What their youth doesn\\u2019t tell you is the 20 years of experience they have in the form of Vlad and Bogdan Belonozhko. Formerly of 64 Audio, the pair founded Bellos to address some of pro audio\\u2019s most prevalent obstacles: Cost, lengthy turnarounds, strenuous repairs, on-stage comfort and fragile components. With their expertise in 3D-printing and a wealth of proprietary tech, Bellos want to deliver comfy, affordable and long-lasting IEMs quickly to you. Did we mention they sound really good too? Today, we cover their flagship X4: A warm reference that makes $999 a near-bargain. Packaging and Accessories The X4 arrives in modest packaging; a sleeved cardboard box with high-res images and text all around. There isn\\u2019t fancy embossing, textures or foils. It\\u2019s functional, no-frills, and the type of packaging you\\u2019d associate with artist-oriented products. Inside, you\\u2019ll find the X4\\u2019s puck case in bubble wrap. Personally, I\\u2019d have preferred a foam-lined interior with the case sitting in a circular cut-out. Dressing up some of the bare cardboard would\\u2019ve helped the unboxing experience too. But, for the working pro, whose main concern is the monitor reaching them in one piece, Bellos\\u2019 effort here should very well suffice. Thankfully, the X4\\u2019s case is the very opposite of modest. It\\u2019s a metal, puck-shaped chassis with a screw-on top, engraved with the company logo and client\\u2019s name. The case is compact, and the lid has smooth action. And, inside are the X4\\u2019s with its cable tied with a twist tie. Again, as a consumer, a leather strap would\\u2019ve been nice. But, speaking as a drummer and engineer who\\u2019s likely to lose such a strap in the midst of a busy set, I\\u2019d instead suggest a Velcro strap attached to the cable. I\\u2019d love to see something like that in Bellos\\u2019 next product. The case\\u2019s internals are very similar to their partner FiR Audio\\u2019s cases. It has a foam insert with cut-outs for the included wax pick and In-Air Canal tips, which we\\u2019ll get into later. The tips come in 3 sizes; small, medium and large. And, the case has a velvet lining throughout to ensure max safety for the IEMs in storage. The X4 comes with an 8-wire SPC cable with FiR Audio\\u2019s RCX connectors and a right-angled, single-ended 3.5mm plug; both housed in a translucent plastic. Its Y-split and cinch are finished in metallic silver, which nicely match the X4\\u2019s logos. Being a pro-focused product, I\\u2019m not expecting heavy, metal connectors or overt branding. No-frills, it is. But, where it matters \\u2013 the lack of fussy memory wire, weight, flexibility, craftsmanship and finishing \\u2013 I really can\\u2019t fault it. It\\u2019s a well-made, all-ends-met cable that\\u2019s been a joy to use whether behind the console, the drums or my desktop. Plus, with FiR\\u2019s RCX, you\\u2019re also guaranteed secure connections at least 5000 times over. Aesthetics, Build and Wearing Comfort When ordering your Bellos Audio CIEM, you can choose between 3 colors \\u2013 matte black, matte green and matte gray. I went with black for my X4. And, they share a common faceplate design; the Bellos Audio emblem inlaid in metallic silver. Despite the relative lack of options compared to their competitors, I think Bellos\\u2019 clean design looks superb. The matte shells are unique to the industry (apart from Bellos\\u2019 partner FiR Audio), and the silver art contrasts crisply against them. I think the alloy-steel faceplate screws add an industrial aesthetic as well. So unless multi-hued swirls, exotic faceplates or custom artwork are an absolute must for you, I think the Bellos look should perfectly suffice for most. As far as build goes, I cannot fault my X4\\u2019s at all. The matte texture is consistent all throughout, the metallic inlay and alloy-steel screws are sunk-in perfectly, and the faceplates join both shells seamlessly. Additional structures like the In-Air Canal nozzles, the RCX sockets, the tiny ATOM vents and the internal nameplate, which contain the user\\u2019s initials and serial number, are made just as well. Like FiR\\u2019s, Bellos\\u2019 shells are printed with above-average thickness, so they feel reassuringly hefty in the hand. This is the quality you can expect with builders as seasoned in 3D-printing as the Bellos brothers, and any musician who gets their hands on these, I\\u2019m sure, will instantly feel like they\\u2019ve gotten their money\\u2019s worth. Through their proprietary In-Air Canal technology, I believe Bellos Audio have crafted the most comfortable pair of customs I\\u2019ve ever worn. I own somewhere between 20-30, and the X4\\u2019s feel (at least in the inner ear) unlike any of them. In place of a hard, acrylic nozzle is an ultra-pliable, soft silicone; even softer than the 2 silicone customs in my collection. And, it genuinely feels like nothing is in my inner ear. I feel acrylic hanging onto my outer ear, then nada beyond that. The closest comparison I can make is wearing a UIEM with tips too small, so you aren\\u2019t achieving a solid seal. Except here, you\\u2019re achieving full isolation and bass. It\\u2019s, again, a truly unique feeling, and it\\u2019s genuinely one of the most notable innovations I\\u2019ve seen in CIEMs in a long time. Bravo. As far as the outer ear goes, I\\u2019d say the X4\\u2019s are closer to medium-sized customs. They aren\\u2019t as filling as 64 Audio\\u2019s, nor as rounded and inner-ear-dominant as VE\\u2019s or Custom Art\\u2019s. They more so grip the top of the ear without filling the concha bowl too much. And, the inner ear, as said, basically feels nothing. Most importantly, Bellos\\u2019 nanometer-precise 3D-printing means its weight and pressure are distributed as well as possible. It\\u2019s a very comfy custom that I\\u2019ve literally used to mix for 5-6 hours straight before experiencing any discomfort. So, any engineer mixing long shows or working long stretches on the band tour bus should find great comfort with these. The Pro Pitch Bellos Audio prides itself as a brand that puts musicians first, and they\\u2019ve developed a pipeline with this very principle in mind. From their 3D workflow, to their performer-friendly technologies, it\\u2019s clear where their priorities lie. Turnaround Time and Serviceability Bellos Audio guarantees a turnaround time of 5 working days after they receive a usable pair of ear impressions; a far, far cry from the 6-to-8 weeks (at least) most brands will put you through. Taking into account the 2 weeks shipping to and from Bellos, you\\u2019ll get your IEM within 3 weeks of ordering, which is astonishing. You can even shave off an extra week if you already have ear molds in digital, STL form. And, if your earphones ever run into any issues, Bellos\\u2019 customs all have removable, screw-on faceplates. It\\u2019s unlike the rest of the industry, where faceplates are glued on with resin adhesive, then sealed with the shell in lacquer. Customs like that require breaking open, almost, to repair the in-ears, then rebuffing and repolishing after the fact. Bellos\\u2019 design makes repair far easier, far less invasive and far quicker. This means, if your in-ears break just days before a big show, you\\u2019ll likely have them back in time for dress rehearsals. Heck, if you\\u2019re local, you can probably send them in in the morning and have them back by lunch. In-Air Canals This is a Bellos Audio original that seeks to revolutionize custom IEM comfort. Unlike traditional CIEMs that have full solid nozzles, Bellos\\u2019 are cut short, and in their place are stems for silicone tips. This means your tougher outer ear will be covered fully by precisely-printed acrylic, and the only material in your sensitive inner ear is soft, pliable silicone. This not only increases comfort to, again, a vanishing degree, but these soft, malleable tips will also maintain a seal when your ear canal inevitably changes shape whilst talking or singing, which hard acrylic nozzles can\\u2019t do. It\\u2019s also worth noting that the tips included with my X4\\u2019s are a new revision, which Bellos started sending out late last year. They\\u2019re now a matte-black versus the clear originals, and what Bellos have changed is their ability to hold onto the nozzle better. So, the in-ears won\\u2019t fall out with the tips stuck in your ear when you\\u2019re moving on stage. And, they should also provide a better seal, which\\u2019ll lead to stronger isolation and bass response. Again, as someone who\\u2019s spent a decade with customs and seen nothing but incremental shifts, I can confidently say this is one of the biggest technological leaps I\\u2019ve seen in a long, long time; it\\u2019s certainly the most practical. I\\u2019ve been able to pull literal all-nighters mixing and mastering on the X4 with no qualms in comfort. I\\u2019d argue this feature alone is worth considering a Bellos IEM as your next, and it\\u2019s something I look forward to seeing their competitors attempt to replicate. ATOM Venting and Isolation Courtesy of their partners FiR Audio, Bellos\\u2019 customs come equipped with ATOM venting in the form of a tiny hole along the seam of the faceplate. This allows a small amount of air to travel to and from the ear canal, releasing any pressure that may build up there. Because of this, fatigue will set in slower, allowing longer, more comfortable listening. And, it results in airier, more open staging as well. Now, the drawback to ATOM venting is the amount of isolation the in-ear provides. Rather than the -26dB traditional customs give, the X4 only has -17dB. In practice, though, I haven\\u2019t found it to negatively impact my performance behind the console or drum kit. Mixing front-of-house in a venue with a fairly-loud PA system, it only takes low-to-moderate volume to drown the speakers out and focus solely on the in-ear mix. And, on stage, the tiny hint of ambience \\u2013 and I do mean tiny \\u2013 actually helps stoke the feeling of a live performance. So, unless your venue is obscenely loud, or you need the utmost isolation from external noise, the X4\\u2019s -17dB should easily suffice. RCX Connectors All of Bellos\\u2019 customs also come with FiR Audio\\u2019s durable RCX (Rigid Coaxial) connectors. They look similar to the more common MMCX, but they\\u2019re rated at a much higher 5000 mating cycles vs. the MMCX\\u2019s 500 (per Amphenol\\u2019s datasheet). So, they\\u2019re less likely to come loose over time \\u2013 they won\\u2019t come flying off when you\\u2019re performing on stage \\u2013 and you\\u2019re less likely to have to send them back in for repair(s). Connections with RCX are also firm and tactile, which, again, just adds to how solid these X4\\u2019s feel to use. The A6t is a harder-hitting IEM with punchier highs and more elevated lows, while the X4 is gentler and refined. Tonal differences aside, this disparity is down to technical performance too. The A6t has limited dynamic range, meaning it\\u2019s punchy all the time . The X4 is far more capable of setting its energy level in accordance with the track, making it a more accurate engineer\\u2019s monitor. Imaging is the same. The A6t\\u2019s stage stays within the head, while the X4 easily spans outside it. I feel the A6t is more ideal if you\\u2019re a stage performer looking for a more vibrant, exciting in-ear to grip your attention, while the X4 is for the engineer looking for a wider, macro and less-biased view of the mix they\\u2019re working on. Lastly, it shouldn\\u2019t come as a surprise that I consider the X4 the far comfier custom with its In-Air Canals. 64 Audio A18t ($2999) Against the X4, the A18t also comes off more v-shaped; lifted lows (and low-mids), plus sharper highs. It also doesn\\u2019t have as much upper-mid presence as the X4, which tames, say, the upper registers of the piano, the electric guitar or the tenor sax compared to the kick drum or hi-hat. In imaging, the two are quite close. I venture to say the X4 has a bit more width, and its stage feels less full because of its comparatively relaxed lows and low-mids. You\\u2019ll hear more texture across 100-1000Hz on the A18t, because it pushes those frequencies a bit further forward. But, the X4 wins out on sheer top-end extension with its airier, blacker background. You can\\u2019t go wrong with either, in my opinion. It\\u2019ll come down to whose tone you find easier to work with, even if the X4\\u2019s far lower price and comfier In-Air Canals give it an inherent advantage. JH Audio Layla ($2199) The Layla and X4 deliver music in a very similar way; relaxed and unforced in a surround sound setting. Both excel at even, out-of-head imaging that instantly lets you know when you\\u2019ve placed an instrument too far forward or too far back. Where they mainly differ is tone, especially up top. Compared to the X4, the Layla is far more relaxed in its low- and upper treble. The former gives it almost-diffuse transients, where cymbals, hi-hats and sibilants don\\u2019t have as much of an edge as they normally should. They come off slightly out-of-focus. And, it\\u2019s also light in air. There isn\\u2019t as much light illuminating the stage and the outlines of instruments within it. Again, like the A18t, this is a matter of preference. The Layla is stronger if you prefer a mellower, more diffuse sound, and the X4 is more ideal if you prefer tighter, better-outlined instruments and a stronger sense of air. JH Audio Sharona ($2499) Of the in-ears here, the Sharona is most like the X4 tonally and technically. Like the Layla, these two have that laidback, unbiased, macro view of music in common. But, the Sharona\\u2019s tonality is much more akin to the X4\\u2019s. The main differences I hear are heavier lows and calmer high-mids on the JH. In a way, it\\u2019s the X4\\u2019s earthier, less dainty sibling. Technically, the Sharona is notably ahead. It resolves textures to a higher degree, and it\\u2019s got a blacker, deeper background, which makes its notes more 3D. This is partly due to its deeper stage too. So, if you have the cash, the Sharona is the stronger mastering IEM, simply due to how much detail it\\u2019s capable of extracting. But, the X4 is one heck of a second option at less-than-half the cost, along with tech like In-Air Canals and ATOM, which, I can say from experience, come in really handy when you\\u2019re mixing for long stretches of time. Verdict Bellos Audio\\u2019s X4 is the engineer\\u2019s in-ear. At $999 MSRP, not only are you getting a monitor that performs far above its price tag, but also an experience tailor-made for the working pro. From its 5-day turnaround, to its nanometer-precise build, peerless comfort, robust connector system and repairability, it\\u2019s astounding that sound quality is just one of the many boxes it deftly ticks. While IEMs are often far down a musician\\u2019s shopping list, and a grand is more than many are willing to spend, if you\\u2019re gonna splurge, \\u2018might as well do it on the one that\\u2019ll give you the utmost for your money. As their flagship, the X4 is a perfect personification of Bellos Audio\\u2019s ethos: No needless frills or bells-and-whistles; just an exceedingly capable and reliable tool that makes you better at what you do. Now, it is time to introduce the new flagship: SE6 Nebula. As the name indicates, the new monitor has 6 drivers, just like the previous flagships of the company. SE6 Nebula keeps the house sound signature of Spiral Ear, which is an important point I believe. Brief Sound Impressions: It is possible to describe SE6 Nebula, briefly, as a spacious, very resolving and natural sounding monitor. The bass is not very prominent, but the preferred amount of the bass in the tuning helps the monitor to maintain a remarkable stage structure. The resolution of the mid-bass is exceptionally good and the sub-bass hits slightly hard but it is never overpowered. The general approach in the bass region leans towards being a bit neutral, rather than romantic or analytic. It is not a bass heavy or shy monitor. The midrange is neither recessed nor forward; its position is exactly where it should be pursuant to my preferences (the source ability is important as well). The resolution is top notch with the exceptional harmonic details. I wouldn\\u2019t describe the overall midrange tonality as \\u2018\\u2019fully warm\\u2019\\u2019, but it can be evaluated as a minimally colored sounding monitor with the traces of the Spiral Ear sound signature. The note releasing is neither very thick nor thin; there is a very good balance in order to maintain a bodied sound and create an impressive resolution at the same time. The treble of SE6 is slightly prominent and a bit thin, but it becomes never edgy, bright or piecing. In my opinion, the right word for the treble presentation should be \\u2018\\u2019alive\\u2019\\u2019. The overall high frequency resolution is high and it shows its full potential when the monitor is connected to a high-quality source. The soundstage is truly remarkable. The space cleanliness around instruments is superb, which results in a top-notch separation and a vivid positioning.\\xa0Additionally, the monitor has a very good layering ability. SE6 Nebula is priced at 2999 EUR without VAT, 3689 EUR with VAT. According to the website, it is only in custom shape. EMIT: Product(source=None, manufacturer=None, name=u'64 Audio N8: Heart and Soul', sku=None, ssid=u'64-audio-n8-heart-and-soul', description=None, category=u'Custom In-Ear Monitor / CIEMs', url=u'https://theheadphonelist.com/64-audio-n8-heart-and-soul/', properties=[], reviews="[Review(title=u'64 Audio N8: Heart and Soul', date=u'2024-06-03', product=None, ssid=u'64-audio-n8-heart-and-soul', url=u'https://theheadphonelist.com/64-audio-n8-heart-and-soul/', click_url=None, super=None, type='pro', authors=[Person(name=u'Guy Lerner', email=None, url=u'https://theheadphonelist.com/author/gler/', ssid=u'gler')], properties=[ReviewProperty(name=None, value=u'My favourite gear reviews are those with an interesting backstory, something to pique my interest beyond the bread-and-butter of describing sound reproduction. Few have piqued my interest more than 64 Audio\\u2019s N8 , an IEM I have long coveted for its bass-first allure as much as anything else it promised sonically. For those who aren\\u2019t aware, my audiophile journey has generally centred on bass. Powerful, textured, emotive bass that contrasts with sweet, seductive vocals and sparkling strings is what\\u2019s always connected me emotionally to music, as far back as I can remember. In audio gear, that emotional connection has more often than not depended on dynamic driver bass. That woosh of air, the vacuum-like suck out which follows an explosive drum hit, the textured ripple of a bass guitar string you can feel in your cheekbones; these are the qualities I find dynamic drivers do better than any other. N8, co-developed by legendary bassist Nathan East, has always seemed to me like a \\u2018love letter\\u2019 to dynamic driver bass in an IEM. It\\u2019s an IEM built to first and foremost deliver the most true-to-life bass tones (and impact) for monitoring and recording a live-performing bassist. But being a 64 Audio IEM, and placed bang in the middle of the company\\u2019s premium, high-performance custom IEM line-up, it\\u2019s invariably designed to be great at conveying much more than just bass. Indeed, N8 is a nine -driver hybrid: a 9mm bass dynamic driver paired with eight custom balanced armatures, seven of which take charge of low-mids to mid-highs, and one of which, 64 Audio\\u2019s signature Tia high-frequency driver, included solely for delivering clear, extended upper treble harmonics. This review is therefore going to meander a bit more than usual. I\\u2019m going to cover some of the history behind this iconic IEM (this year it turns six!), then delve into 64 Audio\\u2019s custom IEM process ( with the help of the knowledgeable and ever-willing Fil Sklyarov ), before getting down to the brass tacks of testing and dissecting sound performance and comparisons. Feel free to jump to whichever sections interest you most, but I invite you to join me for the full tour if you\\u2019re able. I promise to keep it as interesting for you as it was for me. Nathan East and the birth of \\u2018Nate\\u2019 From his early years in Philadelphia to his \\u2018big break\\u2019 as a session musician with Barry White at the tender age of 16, Nathan East is today considered one of America\\u2019s foremost bassists. He is one of the most recorded bass artists in history, with over 2,000 recordings spanning multiple genres, from Jazz to R&B and rock. Phil Collins, Michael Jackson, Daft Punk, Whitney Houston and Stevie Wonder are just some of the music industry legends East has collaborated with in a diverse Grammy Award-winning career that includes numerous solo recordings and countless session recordings. He is also one of the founding members of the popular jazz collective Fourplay. It\\u2019s hard to think of a more apt collaborator, then, for a signature custom IEM, designed with professional touring and recording bassists \\u2013 and, naturally, bass enthusiasts \\u2013 in mind. East is not new to the world of signature audio equipment collaborations, having put his name to a range of Yamaha bass guitars, for example. From what I understand, he therefore had a good understanding of the design process for N8, and was given extensive creative control to determine the sonic direction of the final product. The result is a custom IEM for stage performance and monitoring that\\u2019s equally adept at recreational music enjoyment at the highest level. There is a distinct soulfulness to the sound that, having listened to many of East\\u2019s recordings, are vividly reproduced through this IEM in a way that\\u2019s very different to how I hear them with others, a sound that goes to the heart of the music he\\u2019s creating, which I\\u2019ll touch on in more detail later on. For now, here\\u2019s a short video that 64 Audio and Nathan East put together to tell their story of \\u2018Nate\\u2019, which is well worth watching before we continue. EMIT: Product(source=None, manufacturer=None, name=u'64 Audio N8: Heart and Soul', sku=None, ssid=u'64-audio-n8-heart-and-soul', description=None, category=u'Custom In-Ear Monitor / CIEMs', url=u'https://theheadphonelist.com/64-audio-n8-heart-and-soul/', properties=[], reviews="[Review(title=u'64 Audio N8: Heart and Soul', date=u'2024-06-03', product=None, ssid=u'64-audio-n8-heart-and-soul', url=u'https://theheadphonelist.com/64-audio-n8-heart-and-soul/', click_url=None, super=None, type='pro', authors=[Person(name=u'Guy Lerner', email=None, url=u'https://theheadphonelist.com/author/gler/', ssid=u'gler')], properties=[ReviewProperty(name=None, value=u'Closing thoughts It took me a while to get there, but once I finally heard N8 for myself, almost all my expectations of what it would actually sound like were realised. As a bass connoisseur, there is nothing I appreciate more in an IEM. But great bass alone is not enough at this level, not when you\\u2019re investing in higher-end sets like N8 and demand premium performance. This, to me, is N8\\u2019s true worth: it\\u2019s ability to combine a warm, lush, bass-filled presentation with clarity, extension, a generous stage, precise imaging, and extended fatigue-free listening. Summing up its strengths, besides the obviously sumptuous bass and overall tonal richness, this is a monitor that delivers on the promise of true-to-life reproduction snugly wrapped up in a warm comforter. There\\u2019s no getting around the fact that bass comes first, and basshead-level bass too, especially if you\\u2019re brave enough to swap in the m20. If you\\u2019re not into bass, prefer your bass neutral, or need a bass presentation that\\u2019s subservient to everything else, this is definitely not the IEM for you. On the flip side, we live in a golden age of IEMs, where technical mastery is often favoured ahead of tonal preferences. N8 is not going to compete with summit-fi technical sets, and it probably won\\u2019t win a straight technical race with other IEMs in its class either. That\\u2019s not to say it isn\\u2019t technically competent \\u2013 it very much is \\u2013 but it unapologetically puts tone before tech, so keep that in mind. Another consideration, of course, is that N8 is only available as a custom IEM . While customs are, on the whole, more comfortable, more isolating, and more consistent-sounding than their universal counterparts, they\\u2019re also harder to resell (should you ever need to) because they\\u2019d need to be re-shelled for the buyer at considerable expense. There\\u2019s also no guarantee of a first-time (or even second-time) fit, although 64 Audio\\u2019s generous fit guarantee at least somewhat reduces the risk. Then there\\u2019s the competition. Six years ago, when N8 was first released, there weren\\u2019t too many \\u2013 if any \\u2013 warm tuned, bass-first IEMs that could directly compete with what it does. Today, while I still haven\\u2019t heard an IEM that\\u2019s directly equivalent, there are valid alternatives, some of which like Empire Ears\\u2019 Legend X and Sony\\u2019s Z1R, I haven\\u2019t covered in this review but are well worth considering. But none of these IEMs are N8 . There\\u2019s a story here that\\u2019s worth telling, a heritage of design that, if it\\u2019s meaningful enough to you, you won\\u2019t find anywhere else. N8 is one of those unique IEMs that, for me, has a place in any collection that values a bass-first, easy listening IEM with a vibrant heart and musical soul. As such, it earns my highest possible recommendation . N8 is available to buy direct from 64 Audio for $1,699, excluding premium options.
